Katalog Berita (Halaman 43/57)

#2883. Luka Dončić says he asked Lakers front office to acquire Walker Kessler: "I went to them and said, 'I want Walker"'

One of the Los Angeles Lakers' biggest offseason moves is paying off big so far — and Luka Dončić is taking a bit of credit for making it happen.

Lakers center Walker Kessler, who was acquired from the Utah Jazz in a sign-and-trade deal in July, has been a key piece of the team on both ends of the court so far this season. And as it turns out, Dončić told ESPN's Dave McMenamin that he played a role in Kessler's arrival in L.A.

In McMenamin's profile on Kessler published Friday, Dončić said that he spoke to Rob Pelinka, the Lakers' president of basketball operations and general manager, during the offseason about acquiring the big man from the Jazz. Dončić said that he was inspired by Kessler's impressive defense at the rim after playing against him in previous seasons.

"I told them after the season I wanted Walker," Dončić told ESPN, speaking about that offseason conversation. "I think he's a really good player … Obviously, last season he didn't play. He played like five games. Obviously [Utah] kept him out, but just playing against him in the previous years, it was hard to score. He was protecting the rim, he was dominating the paint. So just after the season, I went to them and said, 'I want Walker.'"

Dončić credits his burgeoning chemistry with Kessler as a key piece of the team's growth. When speaking about his pick-and-roll with Kessler, Dončić told ESPN: "When we get good at that … we can be really good."

In response, Kessler told ESPN that he was honored to have Dončić want him as a teammate.

"To have someone like that, with that pedigree, to say, 'Hey, I want that to be our center'... it was incredible," Kessler said. "I was like, 'Wow, this dude, he believes in me. He knows that I can help this team.' It makes you excited to play."

In many ways, the Kessler trade was risky: The Lakers sent two unprotected first-round picks (2031 and 2033) and two first-round swaps (2028 and 2030) for the 25-year-old big man, who only played in five games last year after undergoing season-ending shoulder surgery in November 2025.

But so far, the move has paid off. Kessler is averaging team-leading 10.8 rebounds and 2.1 blocks per game, and is the Lakers' third-highest scorer with 14 points per game. His 66.9% field-goal percentage is one of the best in the NBA.

It's not a huge surprise that Dončić, who is the Lakers' last remaining superstar after the offseason departure of LeBron James, has filled in the role of player-GM. Dončić is the face of the team and the leading scorer, and seems to have gained significant influence in the Lakers front office.

After the Kessler trade went through in July, Dončić said at the time that he was "excited" about the addition. Reports at the time indicated that the team was following Dončić's two biggest requests, which were to retain Austin Reaves (who signed a four-year, $185 million extension this summer) and bring in a rim protector like Kessler.

The trio of Dončić, Reaves and Kessler have led the Lakers to a 15-3 record to start the season, putting them at the top of the Western Conference.

#2881. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for smaller loan exemption from appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, the Federal Reserve Board, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that the 2027 threshold for higher-priced mortgage loans that are subject to special appraisal requirements will increase from $34,200 to $35,500.

The threshold amount will be effective January 1, 2027, and is based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W, as of June 1, 2026.

The Dodd-Frank Act added special appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans to the Truth in Lending Act, including that creditors obtain a written appraisal based on a physical visit to the interior of the home before making a higher-priced mortgage loan. The rules implementing these requirements contain an exemption for loans at or below a threshold amount that is adjusted annually to reflect CPI-W increases.

#2879. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for applicability of truth in lending and consumer leasing rules for consumer credit and lease transactions

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau today announced the dollar thresholds used to determine whether certain consumer credit and lease transactions in 2027 are subject to certain protections under Regulation Z (Truth in Lending) and Regulation M (Consumer Leasing).

By law, the agencies are required to adjust the thresholds annually based on the annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W. Transactions at or below the thresholds are subject to the protections of the regulations.

Specifically, based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the CPI-W as of June 1, 2026, Regulation Z and Regulation M generally will apply to consumer credit transactions and consumer leases of $76,300 or less in 2027. However, private education loans and loans secured by real property, such as mortgages, are subject to Regulation Z regardless of the amount of the loan.

Federal Register notice: Consumer Leasing (Regulation M) (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Truth in Lending (Regulation Z) (PDF)

#2871. Yahoo Perfect 5: Victor Wembanyama, Giannis Antetokounmpo lead lineup with piece of every team for Friday's Prime doubleheader

Yahoo Sports is giving fans another way to play fantasy basketball with Perfect 5, a free-to-play game featuring each week's NBA on Prime doubleheader. The rules are simple: pick five players, stay within a $15 budget and rack up fantasy points through scoring, rebounds, assists, defensive stats and 3s.

Friday's slate is an NBA Cup Group Play doubleheader: Heat at Knicks (7 p.m. ET) and Lakers at Spurs (9:30 p.m. ET). The injury report is busy again. Karl-Anthony Towns was downgraded to questionable with an abdominal contusion, Andre Drummond is out in concussion protocol and OG Anunoby is out with a calf strain. Giannis Antetokounmpo is probable with groin tendinitis, Austin Reaves is back from a heel bruise and De'Aaron Fox is questionable with a shin injury.

Using Yahoo projections, each player's last five games, the betting lines and the latest injury news, I ran 20,000 simulations of the slate against a simulated Yahoo field built from this week's pick rates. I'm pairing the two best fantasy players on the board and spreading the rest of the lineup across all four teams, so I have a stake in every game.

This group finished in the top 1% of the simulated field 23.4% of the time and beat every entry in the field in 1.49% of sims, and nobody in the simulated field had these exact five. It holds up whether Towns plays or not: 24.3% of the time in the sims where he played and 21.6% when he sat.

Wembanyama is the top projection on the board at 58.17 fantasy points. Over his last five games, he's averaged about 68 fantasy points, including 50 points, 18 rebounds, 4 steals and 5 blocks against the Pelicans last Friday, and 44 points with 16 rebounds at Portland on Wednesday. The blocks are what separate him: he's averaging 3.8 a game, and every one is worth three points.

You'll want some star exposure in a matchup featuring two of the NBA's best teams. San Antonio is a 6.5-point home favorite, and the 226.5 total is the higher of the two games. Wemby was in the single best lineup in 58% of my sims, more than anyone else. Yes, 61% of the field has him, but fading a player this far ahead of everyone else isn't worth it.

This is the pick that makes the lineup. Giannis is projected for 53.67 fantasy points, essentially even with Luka Dončić, but he's on 7% of rosters to Dončić's 58%. He's been a beast in Miami — scoring about 59 fantasy points a game over his last five, including 34 points, 14 rebounds, 8 assists, and 3 blocks against Detroit, and 32 points and 14 rebounds at Philadelphia on Wednesday.

He came into this game probable with a groin injury, but that shouldn't be an issue since he played 36 minutes against Philly a couple of days ago. And the Knicks' frontcourt looks weaker than usual: Drummond is out, Anunoby is doubtful and Towns is a game-time call.

Thybulle is the next best defensive-stats play on the slate to Wemby. He's averaging 2.3 steals and a block, and he's turned that into about 26 fantasy points a game over his last five, with at least 2 steals in four of them. He's added some offense too, scoring 14 points in each of his last two games. Reaves's return could trim his minutes a little, but his value comes from defense, not shots, and that holds up even in a smaller role.

Shamet gets this lineup into the early game without paying for a Knicks star whose status is up in the air (Towns). Shamet's averaging about 19 fantasy points over his last five, right on his 19.50 projection. He's played 30-plus minutes in three of his last four games. He should be in more Sixth Man of the Year conversations and, at 11% picked, he's also a cheaper way to get leverage than Klay Thompson, who's on 58% of rosters at the same $2 cost.

Conwell is the value that makes two $5 stars work. He's projected for 19.25 fantasy points at $1, the best points-per-dollar mark on the slate, and he's played 25-30 minutes in four of his last five games. He doesn't need a big night; 15 to 20 fantasy points from your $1 slot is plenty. He showed up in the optimal lineup in 51% of my sims, more than any player except Wembanyama. The rookie is gaining confidence by the game, so I'll keep riding the wave.

Dončić is projected for 53.95 fantasy points, but that number was built during a stretch where he's carried the Lakers by himself. Reaves is back Friday after missing five games, and some of the shots and touches Dončić has been soaking up will go back to his running mate. I trimmed his projection about 5% in the sims to account for that. Even at full strength, he'd be a tough sell: he costs the same $5 as Giannis, projects for about the same and is on eight times as many rosters.

Very close. Using Yahoo's projections, the highest-scoring possible lineup is Wembanyama, Dončić, Thompson, Thybulle and Conwell at 173.75 fantasy points. Mine comes in just 2.04 points behind it.

The max lineup finished in the top 1% only about 12% of the time, roughly half my lineup's rate. Swapping Dončić for Giannis and Thompson for Shamet gives up two projected points and gets you out of the game's most crowded build.

You can start building your Perfect 5 roster for Friday's slate now, including going up against me in my Perfect 5 group.

#2869. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2867. Nvidia chip buyers told to rule out Chinese renters to purchase GPUs, sources say

Neoclouds are being told they won't be able to purchase Nvidia chips if any of the computing capacity will be rented by Chinese companies, sources told CNBC.

It comes after a new rule restricting remote access to artificial-intelligence semiconductors was announced by the Trump administration on Nov. 16.

Chip sellers communicated the messaging to neoclouds after the announcement, which seeks to close an export controls loophole allowing Chinese companies to rent access to Nvidia's most advanced chips when hosted in data centers overseas, two people familiar with the situation told CNBC. The people asked not to be named when discussing private information.

The interim final rule was announced by the Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, and came into effect on Nov. 19. Previously imposed export controls have effectively banned the sale of the most advanced Nvidia chips to Chinese companies and their import into China.

Prior to the new rule's announcement, Nvidia had said it would not sell its chips to customers renting more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, CNBC reported.

The new rule impacts the full supply chain, from the companies selling Nvidia chips to the neoclouds who rent access to computing power. Neocloud stocks have come under renewed pressure lately as investors questioned the sustainability of the AI boom.

Nvidia and the U.S. Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The change has prompted Nvidia chip sellers to look for new buyers of the semiconductor giant's GPUs, CNBC reported on Wednesday, citing three people familiar with the matter. Two of those people said it appeared chip sellers were seeking to offload GPUs because they were originally for use in data centers through which Chinese companies would rent access.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that the new BIS rule was intended to close "loopholes."

"Some rules were issued by the Commerce Department last week to close off indirect access, either smuggling through places like Southeast Asia or remote access from data centers in third countries," he said.

U.S. lawmakers have been looking to clamp down on China's access to advanced compute.

Separately, Nvidia warned its customers that the proposed Remote Access Security Act — which has passed the House of Representatives but is yet to pass the Senate — would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services, potentially exposing both parties to "legal action," sources previously told CNBC.

#2863. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit on Thursday. The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, the Netherlands' Klaas Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

#2862. Lakers must be aggressive, plug biggest hole that could sink title chances

The Lakers are championship contenders. Luka Doncic is leading the MVP race. If the GM plays his cards right, his team could be in a position to hang its 18th banner.

But first, the Lakers need to address a glaring hole.

Brook Lopez #11 of the LA Clippers and Isaiah Stewart #28 of the Memphis Grizzlies battle for the ball on Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026, in Memphis, Tenn. Getty Images

They need a backup center. They're dangerously thin at that position, an issue that could be a fatal flaw in a playoff series.

You know who could be an intriguing option? Brook Lopez.

The Clippers are sellers. They're open for business. Their players could be dealt at bargain rates.

With Kawhi Leonard, James Harden and Ivica Zubac gone, and the Clippers ravaged by the fallout from the Aspiration scandal, including losing five future first-round draft picks, the team is in full rebuild mode with the goal of trying to get draft assets.

That means Lopez could be prime for the picking.

The 7-foot-1 Lopez won a championship with the Bucks in 2021. He's averaging 14.4 points (55% shooting from the field, 43.3% from beyond the arc), 5.1 rebounds and 1.8 assists per game for the Clippers this season.

He'd give the Lakers a great option behind Walker Kessler. He's a strong rim protector. His efficient outside shooting could especially be an asset against someone like Victor Wembanyama, helping draw him out of the paint.

Lopez is 38. The Lakers may want to acquire a younger center who could be a mainstay on the roster alongside Doncic, Kessler and Austin Reaves in the future. And Lopez's defensive mobility leaves a bit to be desired when compared with younger, more athletic bigs.

But the Lakers are in win-now mode, and Lopez could undoubtedly help tip them over the edge this season.

Brook Lopez of the LA Clippers drives to the basket against the Memphis Grizzlies in Memphis, Tenn., Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

This much is for sure: The Lakers need to be aggressive ahead of February's trade deadline.

They have the NBA's best record at 15-3 They're the it team. These opportunities don't come around often.

It has been six years since the Lakers won their last championship in 2020. Before that? Ten years had elapsed following their title in 2010.

Sacramento Kings defender Dylan Cardwell plays defense against Los Angeles Lakers player Walker Kessler on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

"We'll be active at the trade deadline to shore up things that we see," Pelinka said on Spectrum SportsNet. "We think this team has a real chance to make a run, so we want to give the group as much talent as we can.

"Don't want to get too far over our skis with a hot start, because you don't want to get big-headed. But clearly the way it's gone so far, it's been great. And we hope it continues."

Pelinka spent the offseason building a roster around Doncic, including emptying his treasure chest to acquire Kessler in a sign-and-trade with the Jazz, giving them two first-round picks and two first-round pick swaps on top of a four-year, $130 million deal.

The Kessler gamble has panned out. He's a double-double machine. They found their franchise center. But the Lakers are paper thin behind him, with Kevon Looney falling out of the rotation and Jarred Vanderbilt giving bigger teams an advantage.

And with their neighbors down the street having a yard sale, Lopez could be a good option for a Lakers team with limited draft capital following the Kessler deal.

The Lakers have taken the league by storm this season. They're the real deal. They have a legitimate shot at winning the Larry O'Brien Championship Trophy.

They just have to make sure they plug any holes that could sink them.

#2854. Steelers defense scores two touchdowns in dominant win over Broncos

After a tumultuous week of horrible PR featuring Aaron Rodgers being released from the team, the Pittsburgh Steelers bounced back after two consecutive losses in the biggest way possible, scoring two defensive touchdowns en route to a 27-5 win over the Denver Broncos.

The Steelers' defense set the tone early on. After the offense punted following an eight-play drive, Bo Nix dropped the ball after a pump fake, which rolled into the end zone and was picked up by T.J. Watt for a touchdown to put the Steelers up 7-0. Denver then went three-and-out, and a Mason Rudolph hit Germie Bernard on a short crosser that the rookie took for a 58-yard catch-and-run. Three plays later, Rudolph connected with DK Metcalf for a touchdown on a one-on-one fade route over Pat Surtain. The 14-0 lead also marked the first time since 2019 that Pittsburgh scored 14 points in the first quarter.

Pittsburgh's defense wasn't done scoring, though. After a safety and a field goal made the game 14-5, Chris Boswell kicked a field goal with less than two minutes to play in the half, extending the lead to 17-5. On the ensuing drive, Nix threw a pass that looked as if it was intended for Jalen Ramsey, who took the interception 36 yards to the end zone to give the Steelers a 24-5 lead at the half. A Chris Boswell field goal in the fourth quarter would be the final points of the game, as Pittsburgh wins 27-5 and climbs to the No. 6 seed in the AFC.

The Steelers sacked Bo Nix three times and held him to just 169 yards passing on an astoundingly low 4.2 yards per attempt. Denver couldn't run the ball, either, totaling just 73 yards on 26 carries. Overall, Denver had just 205 yards on three yards per play.

Mason Rudolph didn't throw the ball a ton, but he made his throws count. He finished the day 9-of-15 for 142 yards and a touchdown. He had completions of 58, 37, and 22 yards, pushing the ball downfield and proving to be a steady hand to complement the run game.

The Steelers ran the ball often, totaling 117 yards on 31 carries. It wasn't an explosive run game, but it was effective to help Pittsburgh get a much-needed win.

Pittsburgh now prepares for a massive Week 13 Sunday night game against the Houston Texans, who are fighting for their playoff lives at 4-6.

#2853. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#2849. Judge blocks FCC early-renewal process for Disney-owned ABC stations, for now

A judge has temporarily blocked the Federal Communications Commission from continuing its early renewal of a set of broadcast station licenses for Disney-owned ABC.

Judge Loren L. AliKhan of U.S. District Court in Washington granted Disney the preliminary injunction it sought and denied the FCC's attempt to dismiss the suit.

"This is not a close case," wrote the federal judge. "Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face—loss of their broadcast licenses—the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness."

The broadcast network had filed the lawsuit and followed the FCC beginning its early review of the ABC licenses. The government agency cited concerns around ABC's diversity, equity and inclusion efforts as the reason behind the start of the early renewal — years ahead of their scheduled expiration.

"Today's decision is an important victory for the First Amendment," said an ABC spokesperson.

An FCC spokesperson said in a statement that the part of the decision granting ABC's motion "is wrong on the facts and wrong on the law," the Associated Press reported.

In her ruling, AliKhan said that early license renewal proceedings came over a year into the FCC's enforcement push against ABC, starting with an investigation into Disney's employment practices in March 2025 and leading to the renewal proceedings in April this year.

ABC on Tuesday had said that it continued to "face irreparable harm" from the administration, which "has been acting" through the FCC, which is run by President Donald Trump appointee Brendan Carr.

AliKhan, a Joe Biden appointee, noted in her ruling that ABC had to change how it handled guest appearances for top shows "The View" and "Jimmy Kimmel Live!" with no candidate for public office appearing on the former and a senatorial candidate's appearance on the latter uploaded to YouTube instead of being broadcast.

"The prospect of license non-renewal has also factored into plaintiffs' calculus of when and how they cover President Trump and political topics," said the judge.

ABC had alleged that Trump and Carr have been making comments "intended to chill their protected speech," and that such comments have escalated since the 2024 presidential election.

The White House and the FCC didn't immediately respond to requests for comment from CNBC.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to the story

#2846. T.J. Watt, Steelers defense make statement in first game without Aaron Rodgers

PITTSBURGH – The Steelers punctuated a wild week that included the furious departure of future Hall of Fame quarterback Aaron Rodgers with an exclamation point on Black Friday.

Embattled coach Mike McCarthy's team secured an impressive 27-5 win against the Denver Broncos on Nov. 27 at Acrisure Stadium.

The defense generated a pair of touchdowns while Mason Rudolph, Rodgers' backup the past couple of seasons, led the offense to enough points to cruise amid struggles from the Broncos' own signal caller, Bo Nix.

Here's USA TODAY's report card on the action from a Pittsburgh perspective.

Required reading: Steelers fans spare no one amid Aaron Rodgers, Mike McCarthy feud

McCarthy didn't ask Rudolph to do too much within the game plan, and he didn't really have to as the defense led the scoring. Rudolph completed nine of 15 passes for 142 yards and a touchdown. Most of the throws were on the shorter and safer side, though he did seem more comfortable and willing to stand in the pocket to look downfield than Rodgers has been the past couple of weeks. If that continues, it will be a positive development for this offense. At the same time, it's way too soon to declare whether he'll be a better long-term option than Rodgers.

It was a third tepid week in a row for Jaylen Warren and Rico Dowdle, despite McCarthy leaning heavily into the running game by close to a 2-to-1 margin. Dowdle's 27-yard run in the third quarter helped goose the stats to over 100 yards for the day, but he gave the momentum right back by fumbling on the next play. Remove that big gainer from the equation and the rushing average was well below four yards per touch for the duo. Blocking is undeniably a problem, but neither guy is forcing many misses to bail the blockers out either. They've got to be better.

Rookie Germie Bernard generated the explosive play this offense has sorely lacked with a 58-yard catch and run in the first quarter. DK Metcalf later cashed that possession in with a 22-yard touchdown catch from Rudolph that represented his first meaningful first-half production in several weeks. It was an encouraging step forward after last week's multiple altercations with the Philadelphia Eagles that were largely forgotten once the drama with Rodgers began to unfold. Michael Pittman Jr. was rather quiet in his return from injury, but his presence seemed to help get Metcalf into single coverage. Roman Wilson added a 37-yard catch in the fourth quarter that helped salt the victory away.

Pittman's return and the heavy dose of runs marginalized Pat Freiermuth and Darnell Washington in the passing game. Both were among Rodgers' favorite targets, but it remains to be seen how much Rudolph does or does not want to utilize them, especially with his receiving group now mostly healthy for the first time since early in the season.

The protection of Rudolph was pretty good. He was sacked just once and had the luxury of several extended drops. His mobility relative to Rodgers' no doubt helped these guys look better. The run blocking still simply has not found its groove in the three games that left guard Mason McCormick has largely missed. Position coach James Campen has his work cut out for him in getting this group ready for December and January football.

Denver managed just 22 yards on the ground in the first half as the Pittsburgh front flew to the ball and staked itself to a comfortable lead. There were several tackles for a loss mixed in, and that forced Nix to throw more often than Denver probably wanted him to. Tough rush defense was a hallmark of Pittsburgh's four-game winning streak earlier in the season. If the guys up front can maintain this form, it will help as the weather conditions deteriorate down the stretch.

T.J. Watt recovered a fumble in the end zone for a touchdown in the first quarter to loudly announce his presence after a couple of quiet weeks in which he generated just one pressure in the two losses to Cincinnati and Baltimore. He added a fourth-quarter sack. Nick Herbig returned from injury with a key second-quarter sack that pushed the Broncos out of field goal range and thwarted a scoring threat. Payton Wilson and Patrick Queen were active again, each notching tackles for loss while helping the defensive front lock down on the Denver running game.

Cornerback/safety Jalen Ramsey intercepted Nix and returned it 36 yards for a touchdown shortly before halftime. It was one of the biggest plays he's made in Pittsburgh since general manager Omar Khan traded for him before the 2025 season. Asante Samuel Jr. added a fourth-quarter pick that solidified a comfortable lead. Nix generally struggled to make things happen against the Steelers' soft zone coverage scheme. Ramsey and Co. mostly kept the play in front of them and forced Denver to take small chunks at a time without making mistakes. Nix failed in that regard, so it was a nice day for this group that has really only struggled against Cincinnati's Joe Burrow over the past couple of months.

Patrick Graham's defense came ready to play at home. The disruptiveness and splash plays were badly needed and gave this offense room to breathe in front of a home crowd that probably would not have been friendly had Pittsburgh fallen behind. It was hard to see the "spark" McCarthy sought from the offense, though. The passing game still lacks verticality and McCarthy seems to want to force the run when it's not there in the absence of Rodgers to push back on him. For that reason, he's got to own it more than ever when the plan doesn't work particularly well. The offense did score just 13 points, after all.

#2844. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2842. Switzerland’s biggest bank set for showdown with lawmakers as capital fight intensifies amid crisis planning

UBS's crisis planning has won qualified support from market regulators, which called its plan "executable" in principle. But Switzerland's biggest bank still faces a major fight over sweeping plans that would force it to hold more capital against its foreign subsidiaries.

FINMA, the Swiss markets regulator, said Wednesday that UBS's resolvability — or its ability to fail safely, without wreaking havoc on the economy — has improved since it integrated rival Credit Suisse, with progress made in implementing the emergency plan across group-wide resolution planning.

The preferred resolution strategy — involving plans to allow for recapitalization and restructuring through the use of loss-absorbing bail-in bonds — is "executable," FINMA said. The regulator formally approved UBS's recovery plan for the first time since its ​Credit Suisse integration.

How UBS would withstand a future crisis remains in sharp focus among Swiss authorities ever since the lender's emergency takeover of stricken rival Credit Suisse in 2023.

UBS is now the country's only global systemically important bank, and the Swiss government wants it to hold stronger capital cushions, specifically in relation to its foreign subsidiaries, which would help it withstand another banking collapse.

UBS has pushed back against the overhaul, saying the measures are disproportionate and would damage its international competitiveness, forcing it to hold an extra $16 billion in capital.

A parliamentary showdown now looms, with the National Council, Switzerland's lower house, scheduled to debate the proposal Dec. 16.

On Tuesday, the lower house's economics committee backed plans to force the bank to support its foreign subsidiaries with 90% high-quality core Common Equity Tier 1, or CET1, capital. The Council of States, the country's upper house, approved the 90% plan in September.

The Swiss government had earlier proposed a 100% threshold. UBS wants it to be no more than 50%.

UBS CEO Sergio Ermotti told CNBC in an interview Oct. 28 that 90% would not allow the company to be a "competitive player" long-term. But he would not be drawn on speculation that the measures could push the bank to relocate out of Switzerland.

In a statement, the bank said the economics committee's recommendation would herald further excessive tightening of Swiss capital requirements, "which are already among the most stringent globally."

FINMA said in its Nov. 25 update that the goal is to avoid government solvency support and emergency law.

"The aim is to enable the authorities, in the event of a crisis, to choose the option that offers the best prospects of success in safeguarding financial stability and ensuring the continuation of systemically important functions," the regulator said.

#2841. What we learned as Warriors' valiant comeback attempt falls short vs. Blazers

What we learned as Warriors' valiant comeback attempt falls short vs. Blazers originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

During Steve Kerr's pregame news conference before his Warriors faced the Trail Blazers on Friday night in Portland, he raised a caution flag big enough to cover Oregon.

"We're finding our rhythm offensively since the (road) trip where we really struggled," Kerr told reporters at Moda Center. "But this team is a different animal defensively. They're going to test us way more than the last couple games felt. We're going to feel that pressure. We're going to see how we can respond to it."

Not well at all, as the Warriors' one-game road trip ended with a 116-94 loss that eliminated them from NBA Cup competition.

Four Warriors scored in double figures, led by Yaxel Lendeborg's 18 points. De'Anthony Melton had 17, Brandin Podziemski 16 and Charles Bassey 12.

Portland, playing without Damian Lilliard and Ja Morant, limited the Warriors to 36.4-percent shooting from the field, including 33.3 percent from distance.

Here are three observations from the loss that dropped Golden State's record to 10-9:

The same Warriors who have won eight consecutive games at home have lost six straight as visitors because they are not the same Warriors.

The "home" Warriors apply ball movement and accurate shooting, and they turn up their defense when needed. The "visitor" Warriors enter an arena and immediately fall apart on both ends.

Golden State fell behind so quickly and decisively that its 14th point didn't arrive until the game's 15th minute. The Blazers by then had piled up 47 points. The Warriors were singing the same song as on their last road trip, when they lost the last four games by an average of 27.3 points.

The Warriors once again didn't match the energy of the home team, seemingly taken aback by Portland's intensity on both ends. They fell behind by 24 within nine minutes before finding enough offense in the second quarter to trim a 36-point deficit to 23 (67-44) at the half.

The first step to curing this ailment is to start taking their Chase Center vitality on the road.

While the first quarter was reminiscent of Golden State's first quarters on its last road trip, there was a striking difference that might be significant in upcoming games.

This time, the Warriors would not allow themselves to be humiliated into submission.

It was as if looking at the scoreboard early in the second quarter and seeing a 36-point deficit pumped wolverine blood through the Warriors' collective bodies.

Putting some screws into their loose defense and regaining familiarity with the hoop, Golden State won the second quarter by three (33-30) behind an offense buoyed by Melton's three triples.

The Warriors came out after halftime and rallied behind Lendeborg's 12 points in 10 minutes to win the third quarter by 10 (34-24) and enter the fourth trailing 91-78 – close enough for a fighting chance.

Ultimately, with the Blazers regaining their first-quarter zest, it was not enough. For that, the Warriors can blame their first 14 minutes.

Bassey made his fifth consecutive start at center, and there is no reason to believe he will be heading back to the bench anytime soon.

First off, Bassey at center allows Draymond Green the freedom to stray from the paint, defend perimeter players and play the passing lanes. That versatility tends to help the team's overall defense.

Secondly, Bassey's soft shooting touch makes him a potent scoring threat in in the paint. He has scored in double figures in all five starts, averaging 12.2 points per game.

His 12 points on this night came on 6-of-9 shooting from the field. He added five rebounds and is averaging 8.4 per game since moving into the starting lineup.

Bassey continues to work on his screening, which often isn't as bad as it looks. His illegal-screen turnovers should decrease with better timing with his ball-handling teammates.

#2838. Private markets are more accessible than ever for investors. Getting out is another matter

Retail investors are lining up to back private funds. But education on how restrictive these funds can be, particularly on when and how much money they can get back, may not be keeping pace with the influx of new capital.

PwC research has found that 77% of fund distributors surveyed said access to private markets is becoming important or very important to what they plan to offer clients, but just 15% of asset managers said they provide comprehensive education for retail investors.

Unlike the public markets retail investors are more used to, some private-market vehicles limit when investors can get their money back to set repurchase windows, and even then may return only part of what they request.

A spike in redemption requests at several private-credit funds earlier this year exposed the tension between giving investors regular opportunities to cash out and holding assets that can be difficult to sell quickly.

Blackstone restricted withdrawals from its $79 billion flagship nontraded BCRED private credit fund to 5% of shares in June, after redemption requests hit 10% during the second quarter. Apollo also capped withdrawals in its Apollo Debt Solutions vehicle at 5% after second-quarter redemption requests surged to almost 17%. Blue Owl, Morgan Stanley and Partners Group also limited investor withdrawals from some of their funds.

Limitations on redemptions are a key trade-off in structures such as LTAFs in the U.K., ELTIFs in the EU and interval funds in the U.S., which broaden access to less-liquid assets.

It's emerged as a major pressure point as those structures come under closer scrutiny.

"Private markets can have very different characteristics from the products many wealth clients are used to, particularly around liquidity, valuations and holding periods," said Albertha Charles, global asset and wealth management leader at PwC.

"Investors and advisers need to understand how these products work, including their liquidity and valuation characteristics, so they can make informed decisions about whether they are appropriate."

PwC's 2026 Asset Management Revolution report, published Nov. 23, surveyed 250 asset managers, 100 institutional investors and 100 distributors across 36 countries and regions. More than half of respondents represented firms with over $20 billion in assets under management.

Among the study's key findings, education and liquidity are seen as the main hurdles as private markets reach a broader investor base. Distributors said better advisor and client education would help expand access, while liquidity, lock-ups and suitability requirements remain major barriers. Private equity and private credit were also the areas where distributors saw some of the biggest gaps in advisor knowledge.

Private credit appears likely to draw the strongest demand — two-thirds (66%) of distributors expect it to lead client interest in private markets, compared with 46% for private equity.

Regulators in several major markets are creating structures to widen access to long-term and illiquid assets. The U.K. uses long-term asset funds, or LTAFs, while the EU's European long-term investment funds, known as ELTIFs, can be marketed across member states.

In the U.S., interval funds provide periodic, rather than daily, liquidity. Singapore, Japan and Hong Kong have also taken steps to broaden retail or listed access to private assets. The frameworks differ, but all aim to make less-liquid investments workable within existing distribution, suitability and liquidity requirements.

Yen Sum, global chair of the private capital practice at Latham & Watkins, and a member of its executive committee, said access to private-market funds is generally limited to investors who certify they are qualified or sophisticated.

She told CNBC on Thursday that the key issue for investor education centers on how private assets differ in liquidity, maturities and return-of-capital terms, and how higher rates can pressure leveraged companies — rather than simply whether regulation should curb access.

#2833. Rheinmetall reins in growth ambitions as Europe's rearmament boom hits hurdles

Rheinmetall is adopting a more "cautious" approach after the German arms maker lost a major contract to build F126 warships for the country's military, in what would have been Rheinmetall's biggest order since World War II.

The tank and ammunition maker now expects sales of between 33 and 37 billion euros (between $36.2 billion and $40.6 billion) by 2030, down from a previous forecast of about 45 billion euros, it told investors at its Capital Markets Day on Friday.

That guidance incorporates a "more prudent approach" for its large vehicle systems and weapons and ammunition businesses.

CEO Armin Papperger said a 2-billion-euro reduction in the 2030 sales target for its vehicle systems business was a strategic decision to build in a "buffer" against potential project delays and supply chain disruptions, rather than a reflection of further lost contracts or weakening demand.

It follows a full-year 2026 guidance cut earlier this month, which sent shares down 16% as the company cited delays to military contracts and ammunition deliveries.

"The opportunity has not changed; the way we plan for it has," Rheinmetall said in a company presentation.

"We are becoming more cautious whilst retaining our agility in a dynamic market," it said, highlighting a "new normal level of conservatism and recalibrated ambition" in the mid-term.

Shares rose 4% in mid-morning trading amid a European defense sector firmly in the green.

Rheinmetall and the wider defense industry have benefitted massively from European governments' efforts to rearm amid the war in Ukraine and uncertain security guarantees from the U.S. As governments ramp up military spending to meet NATO's hiked spending targets, defense companies see swelling order books but also struggle to meet soaring demand.

While shares of Rheinmetall have averaged triple-digit gains between 2022 and 2025, they are down 49% year-to-date. Addressing capital markets specifically, Papperger said he understood the disappointment, but that the loss of the major F126 was "a political thing."

"The government made this decision, and the minister made that decision," he said.

Other headwinds for Rheinmetall in 2026 were an explosion at an ammunition factory in Murcia, Spain, in January, and delays to Germany's Arminius project, a major military procurement program for Boxer armored vehicles, which is now expected to be commissioned next year.

Papperger said delays were due to price negotiations with the government. "I could have the contract immediately if I would reduce the prices, he said.

"We are fighting hard to get this contract, and we are fighting hard, not only to get the contract, but to make it also profitable."

RBC Capital analysts called the lowered mid-term targets a "widely expected reset" with "a general dose of conservatism."

"[The] emphasis on digitalisation offerings and the broadness of the portfolio should also help address sentiment around Rheinmetall being an "old-tech" name in a "new-tech" paradigm," the analysts added.

Some investors are now questioning what role legacy defense contractors like Rheinmetall, BAE Systems, and Leonardo will play in an evolving military context, increasingly focused on technology, autonomous systems, and drones.

Papperger pushed back on the idea that drones will render traditional military equipment obsolete, arguing that militaries must still replace their aging fleets and maintain the ability to transport troops on the battlefield.

"Don't believe what a lot of people [are] telling you in the narrative 'we only need drones.' Forget about that," Papperger said. "The market potential is still there."

Rheinmetall on Friday also warned that Europe faces mounting security threats beyond conventional warfare, pointing to an increase in hybrid attacks ranging from industrial sabotage and cyber espionage to airspace disruption and satellite jamming.

#2831. Aaron Rodgers-less Steelers end chaotic week with resounding win over Broncos

Mike McCarthy has smiled before on the football field, in the aftermath of a win, in the walk from the sideline to the locker room as the handshakes unfold. But this one, this grin that turned into a beam by the time he left the grass at Acrisure Stadium after the Steelers' 27-5 win over the Broncos on Friday, felt different.

This one, after a week when Pittsburgh staunchly supported McCarthy when Aaron Rodgers — who led him to the Super Bowl in Green Bay, vouched for him to land the Steelers job and then shredded him in an interview that thrust the NFL's model of stability into a state of turmoil — contained a hint of validation. Less than 24 hours after ex-Steelers head coach Mike Tomlin called their present a "dumpster fire," McCarthy and Mason Rudolph, the two members in the spotlight the most after Rodgers' scathing rant Wednesday, proved they might survive the chaos after all.

With Rodgers now released, with signs and pictures of No. 8 removed from around the stadium, with Pittsburgh just over 48 hours removed from what Prime Video broadcaster Al Michaels described as the "shot heard 'round the football world," the Steelers delivered a resounding statement to move to 7-4. Rudolph completed 9-of-15 passes for 142 yards and a touchdown, converting when needed and facilitating a fast start for Pittsburgh. T.J. Watt and Jalen Ramsey returned a fumble and an interception for a touchdown, respectively, and the Steelers defense silenced Bo Nix (22 of 40, 169 yards, two interceptions).

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Mason Rudolph (2) celebrates with Max Iheanachor (71) during an NFL football game in Pittsburgh, Friday, Nov. 27, 2026. AP Photo/David Dermer

"There's a lot going on," McCarthy told reporters. "Any time you're going through a situation where time is taken away from winning and your team responds, it is extremely important and gratifying. You have to make sure you keep it about the players."

Maybe it was the initial roar from the crowd, the one that followed Rudolph's introduction as the starting quarterback, that suggested the Steelers — and, by extension, McCarthy — might survive. Maybe it was the first touchdown pass from Rudolph to DK Metcalf, who'd been ejected from the game last weekend in what ultimately served as one of the final turning points of the Rodgers era in Pittsburgh.

On Monday, after the Steelers dropped a second consecutive game, McCarthy reiterated that Rodgers was his quarterback. One day later, Rudolph was named the Steelers starter and Rodgers was listed as inactive in an 11:10 p.m. X post. Then, on Wednesday, Rodgers went on "The Pat McAfee Show" and claimed that McCarthy fell asleep during meetings, broke down a halftime huddle with "1-2-3 Packers" and missed walk-throughs. Rodgers was released by the Steelers later that day, and the organization has since reiterated its belief in McCarthy.

Rudolph made his 20th career start Friday, but it marked his first since Week 12 of last season. The circumstances surrounding it were shocking too. With their win Friday, the Steelers demonstrated that the trust — in Rudolph by turning to him over young quarterbacks, in McCarthy by keeping him on staff even after the accusations — was warranted. They jumped out to a quick 14-0 lead behind the touchdowns from Watt and Metcalf, and even after the Broncos got on the board with a safety and crept closer with a field goal, Pittsburgh managed to create some breathing room before halftime. Chris Boswell connected on a field goal, and Ramsey returned an errant pass from Nix 36 yards for a touchdown to make it 24-5 with 1:33 remaining in the second quarter.

Pittsburgh Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy walks across the field after an NFL football game against the Denver Broncos in Pittsburgh, Friday, Nov. 27, 2026. AP Photo/Gene J. Puskar

"In a weird way," Rudolph said on the Prime Video broadcast postgame, "the adversity probably brought us closer together."

Neither offense managed much in the second half. Nix struggled the whole game. And Rudolph didn't need to play hero. By that point, the Steelers had already made their statement. By that point, they'd already produced a sample that showed that perhaps the organization isn't scrambling after all, that maybe they can rebound from the chaos Rodgers left them with.

They're now 7-4. They'll be tied with the Bengals atop the AFC North if Cincinnati falls Sunday. And they ended a week of questions with a resounding answer.

"That dude has had to step into some critical situations over his career," defensive tackle Cam Heyward told reporters of Rudolph, "and he's ready for those moments. … He understood that, with everything that went on, he couldn't have a drop-off. And I'm just appreciative to have a guy that s–t hits the fan, he's able to just say, 'All right, I've gotta step up.' "

#2829. German software firm SAP is reportedly cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push

SAP is preparing a plan to eliminate at least 10,000 positions as the German firm reshapes its software business for the artificial intelligence era, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

"The business environment in which we operate is changing rapidly through AI, and we are continuously reviewing how our organization and workforce need to evolve to remain successful," SAP said in a statement to CNBC.

"As part of this ongoing transformation, roles and functions will naturally change over time. Beyond this, no decisions have been made regarding any further measures. We do not comment on speculation."

The company did not provide any further details on potential job cuts.

The report came as the company flagged a cybersecurity attack, but said it had found "no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data."

"SAP has identified suspicious activity and promptly implemented the necessary measures to protect its customers and preserve the security of its services," a company spokesperson said.

"Our customers trust us to protect their data, and that remains our highest priority. In a highly dynamic threat environment, we will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on."

Other software companies have also tied workforce reductions to AI initiatives, though for different reasons. WiseTech Global said in February it planned to cut about 2,000 jobs over two years as it integrated AI into its products and operations.

Atlassian said in March it would cut about 1,600 jobs partly to fund further AI investment, while Cloudflare said in May it would eliminate more than 1,100 roles as it moved toward an AI-first operating model.

In the U.S., employers cited AI in 121,401 job-cut announcements through October 2026, accounting for about 20% of the total, according to a Nov. 5 report from outplacement and executive coaching firm Challenger, Gray & Christmas.

SAP employs more than 110,000 people worldwide. Bloomberg reported that all departments had been asked to identify potential cuts, with product, engineering and sales in particular focus.

The company also underwent a reorganization in 2024, when it eliminated around 10,000 positions through a program that relied mainly on voluntary departures and retraining.

Its headcount later remained relatively flat as it hired for new roles, Bloomberg reported.

In 2023, SAP CEO Christian Klein told CNBC he did not necessarily expect a reduction in the workforce, saying he expected the company to grow and "accelerate" its total revenue.

The reported cuts come as Klein pushes SAP toward an AI-first strategy. This year, SAP completed four acquisitions — Reltio, Dremio, Prior Labs and TechWolf — and made strategic investments in AI-focused companies including Parloa, n8n and Conduct.

The acquisitions are intended to help SAP organize company data, develop AI models for business information and analyze workforce skills.

#2826. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2824. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2823. Brock Purdy receives major injury update ahead of 49ers-Seahawks game

The 49ers won't have quarterback Brock Purdy when they take on the Seahawks on Sunday.

Purdy is dealing with an ankle injury suffered during last week's game against the Vikings and Mac Jones will start in his place, coach Kyle Shanahan announced Friday.

San Francisco 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy reacts before an NFL football game against the Minnesota Vikings in Mexico City, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AFP via Getty Images

Purdy sustained the injury in the second quarter of last week's 35-10 victory over the Vikings in Mexico City. He had his ankles taped for the second half before he finished 21-of-26 passing for 255 yards and three touchdowns while helping solidify his case as an MVP candidate.

Shanahan told reporters it was a mid-ankle sprain that didn't heal as quickly as the team would have hoped. Purdy missed practice the entire week, leaving Jones to receive practice reps with the starters.

"Any time it's not a high ankle sprain, you get pretty relieved. But it was more mid than low, which is a little bit worse," Shanahan said. "(I) kinda had a feeling just the way it was by the time Wednesday came around. So didn't heal up as fast as we wanted so we had to shut him down."

Shanahan was asked if Purdy would be available for the following game when the 49ers travel to play the Giants. He said it was too soon to tell.

"But I'm pretty optimistic about it. But I was about this week to start, and then it changes. Hopefully it'll be all right," he said.

The 49ers signed Jones before the 2025 season to a two-year, $8.4 million contract to upgrade their backup quarterback situation after struggling through stints with Brandon Allen and Joshua Dobbs the season prior.

"Mac's been awesome," Shanahan said. "He's had a great week. We went through this a lot last year, so we got a ton of confidence in Mac. Mac's had a hell of a camp, and he's the same guy every day. So it's been a good week with Mac."

Mac Jones #10 of the San Francisco 49ers attempts a pass against the Minnesota Vikings in Mexico City, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. Getty Images

The 49ers believe Jones is one of the NFL's best backup quarterbacks after relying on him heavily when Purdy was injured in 2025. Teams called about acquiring Jones at the trade deadline, but the price was reportedly too high. The Vikings were reportedly among those teams and instead opted to swap a third-round draft pick with the Raiders for Kirk Cousins.

"Any time you have a backup quarterback you believe could be one of the better starters in this league, that's a hell of a deal," Shanahan said. "It's always hard and tempting when someone offers a lot of stuff, because you know how much that can help your team. But you're kinda glad you don't have to make that decision if because of how bad it is if your starter gets hurt and you're missing that guy. So really grateful that he's here and that we didn't make that trade."

San Francisco 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy listens to a question after an NFL football game against the Dallas Cowboys in Arlington, Texas, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Julio Cortez

San Francisco went 5-3 in Jones' eight starts last season while Purdy was dealing with a turf toe injury suffered in Week 1. Jones completed 69.6% of his passes, averaging 269 yards per game with 13 touchdown passes and six interceptions.

Jones kept the 49ers afloat while they finished 12-5 and made the playoffs. Sunday's game will mark the 11th Purdy has missed due to injury since the start of the 2024 season.
