#2822. Atlanta Hawks (11-6) at Boston Celtics (12-6) Game #19 11/27/26

Atlanta Hawks (11-6) at Boston Celtics (12-6)Friday, November 27, 20268:00 PM ETRegular Season Game #19, Home Game #8TV: NBCSB, ANF/PSN/Prime Video, NBA-LPRadio: 98.5 Sports Hub, 92.9 The Game, Sirius XMTD Garden

The Celtics return home from their 4 game road trip to take on the Atlanta Hawks. This is the first of 3 games between these teams this season. They will play 2 more games in Atlanta on February 15 and April 9. They split the series 2-2 last season with each team winning one at home and one on the road. The Celtics are 247-153 overall, all time against the Hawks. They are 133-46 in games played at Boston.

This is the final NBA Emirates Cup group game for both teams. The Hawks are 2-1 and the Celtics are the only unbeaten team in the East in group play at 3-0. To make the quarterfinals for the first time since 2023, the Celtics need to beat Atlanta because head to head matchup would be the tie breaker should the Hawks win. They could make it into the quarterfinals as the wild card team if their point differential ends up the largest. Currently they have the best point differential at +68. Detroit +31, Miami +25, Cleveland +24 and Atlanta +21 could challenge that with a Celtics loss and a blow out win from any of those teams.

The Celtics finally got a win after 4 straight losses and are now 2nd in the East, 2.5 games behind 1st place Philadelphia and they are tied with 3rd place New York and half a game ahead of 4th place Atlanta. They are 11-2 against Eastern Conference opponents. They are 6-1 at home and 6-4 in their last 10 games. They won their last game. The Hawks are 4th in the East, 3 games behind 1st place Philadelphia and half a game behind 2nd place Boston. They are 10-2 against Eastern Conference teams. They are 6-3 on the road and 8-2 in their last 10 games. They have won their last 4 games.

The Hawks made some changes since last season. They brought in Lugentz Dort, Aaron Wiggins, Devin Carter and Ryan Nembhard through trades. They waived Carter and Nembhard before the season. They drafted Kingston Flemings with the 8th pick in the draft, Zuby Ejiofor with the 23rd pick and Henri Veesaar with the 52nd pick in the draft. They lost Zacharie Risacher, Gabe Vincent, Christian Koloko, Tony Bradley, and Keaton Wallace.

After this game at home, the Celtics will be back on the road playing at San Antonio and Dallas. Then it's home against Charlotte before the break for the NBA Cup tournament. Then, they play a game at Brooklyn before they have a 7 game home stand against Washington twice, Utah, Detroit, Portland, Miami and Cleveland. Then it's back on the road for a 6 game road trip through Chicago, Utah, Portland, Phoenix, the LA Lakers and the LA Clippers.

The Hawks are playing in the first game of a 3 game road trip that will take them to New York and Miami after this game. They then host Indiana before the break for the NBA Cup Tournament. Then they go on a 3 game road trip through Portland, Phoenix and Denver before they host Orlando, Oklahoma City, Detroit and Charlotte. Then they are back on the road to play one game at San Antonio before hosting Chicago, Milwaukee and Dallas.

The Celtics have been rotating players at the shooting guard spot. They have started Jordan Walsh, Hugo Gonzalez, Baylor Scheierman and most recently, Sam Hauser. The starting lineup of White, Hauser, George, Tatum and Queta are a plus-54 per 100 possessions and they got off to an 18-9 start en route to an 80 point first half. However, after the game, Joe Mazzulla was iffy about sticking with this lineup. I'm guessing that Hauser starts again, but he could go back to Walsh, Scheierman or Gonzalez. I've listed the Hawks starters from their game notes.

Mitchell Robinson is the only player on the Celtics injury report at this time and he is once again out due to an illness. The Hawks have 5 players on the injury report. The Celtics need to keep him out of the paint and off the boards, but they can't leave him open on the perimeter because he is capable of hitting 3's. Keys to the GameDefense – Defense is always a key to winning games. The Celtics have an offensive rating of 115.7 (9th) while the Hawks have an offensive rating of 112.7, which is 20th. The Celtics have a defensive rating of 105.8 which is 2nd while the Hawks have a defensive rating of 109.7, which is 6th. The Celtics have been hot and cold on defense this season. Most of the time they can shut down the other team but sometimes they allow them to score at will like allowing the Magic to score 40 in the 4th quarter or the lowly Kings to score 35 in the 3rd quarter. The Celtics need to defend the perimeter because the Hawks are 4th, shooting 43.3 threes a game. They also have to defend the paint as the Hawks are 6th with 51.2 points in the paint per game. The Celtics must make defense a priority in this game and they have to play that tough defense for 48 minutes and not let up.

Rebound – Next to defense, rebounding is the key to winning. When the Celtics put out the extra effort on the boards, it usually carries through to the rest of their game. The Hawks are averaging 45.7 rebounds per game (5th) while the Celtics are averaging 45.2 rebounds per game (10th). The Celtics need to aggressively go after every rebound. They can't afford to give the Hawks extra possessions and 2nd chance points by allowing them to beat them to rebounds. The Hawks are 5th with 16.9 second chance points per game and so the Celtics must keep them off the offensive glass.

Effort and Focus for 48 Minutes– The Celtics have to play with extra effort overall for all 4 quarters. In most of their losses and even in some of their wins, they have allowed their opponents to play with more energy than them for periods of time during the game. They play well for stretches but let up and allow their opponents to surge ahead, especially in the 3rd quarter. The Celtics offensive rating in the 3rd quarter is 96.9 and their defensive rating in the 3rd quarter is 124.8, giving them a net rating of -27.9, which is the 2nd worst in the league. They have to stay focused for all 48 minutes and be ready for their opponents to play harder in the second half and they need to match that effort. They also have to come out with more effort and energy to start the game and not dig themselves into a hole. The Hawks have been playing very well and so the Celtics will need maximum effort for all 48 minutes to beat them.

Move the Ball Carefully – When the Celtics move the ball and find the open man, they are tough to beat. When they hold the ball and try to do too much individually, they become predictable and struggle. The Hawks move the ball well and average 30.3 assists per game while the Celtics are 16th with 26.7 assists per game. The Celtics need to move the ball, but they have to make careful passes because the Hawks are 2nd in the league with 22.6 points off turnovers per game.

X-FactorsHome Game and Cup Game– They call it home court advantage for a reason and the Celtics need to take advantage of playing on their home court. The Celtics need to get motivation from the home crowd, which should be loud and doing everything in their power to support the Celtics and rattle the Hawks. However, it is the first game at home after a long road trip and those games are tough on the home team. The Hawks have the distractions of travel, an unfamiliar arena and a hostile crowd and hopefully that will give the Celtics an advantage. The Hawks have been off since Sunday and so may also have some rust to deal with. Both teams should have extra motivation to win this game in order to advance to the quarterfinals of the NBA Cup.

Officiating – Officiating is always an x-factor in every game. Every crew officiates differently. Some call it tight, others let them play. The Celtics need to adjust to how the refs are calling the game and not allow bad calls or no calls to take away their focus from playing the game the right way. We have seen how much of an x-factor officiating can be in a few games this season. The Celtics have to play so well all game that the officiating, no matter how bad, can't influence the outcome.

#2819. Lakers' Austin Reaves available for NBA Cup matchup vs. Spurs, returning after 5-game absence

Austin Reaves will return to the Los Angeles Lakers' lineup for their NBA Cup matchup with the San Antonio Spurs on Friday. He is listed as available on the team's injury report.

Reaves, 28, has been sidelined since suffering a bruised heel in a Nov. 11 win over the Milwaukee Bucks. At the time, the Lakers believed Reaves' injury wasn't serious and didn't require imaging. Head coach JJ Redick said the guard was day-to-day. However, he missed the team's next five games.

During that five-game span, the Lakers went 4-1 while facing lesser teams including the Charlotte Hornets (4-13), Utah Jazz (3-16) and Sacramento Kings (2-16).

In 12 games this season, Reaves is averaging 24.7 points per game, second behind Luka Dončić, who leads the league with an average of 36.6 points. He's also averaging 4.7 assists and 1.2 steals while shooting 41% on 66 3-point attempts.

The Spurs also could get guards De'Aaron Fox and Dylan Harper back for Friday's game. Both are listed as questionable.

The Lakers go into Friday's matchup with the NBA's best record at 15-3, a half-game ahead of the 15-4 Spurs.

In the NBA Cup standings, both teams have a 2-1 record in the Western Conference's Group C. San Antonio would win the group with a win and the Portland Trail Blazers losing to the Golden State Warriors. However, with a +48 point differential, the Spurs could still advance on a tiebreaker.

The Lakers would prevail with a victory and a Warriors loss. But a defeat would eliminate them from the in-season tournament.

#2817. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#2814. Two Italians killed in Kuwait drone attack; Trump says Iran war ends ‘whenever I want it’

Two Italians have been killed after an alleged Iranian attack on Kuwait, Kuwaiti forces said Thursday.

In a post on X, Kuwait's army said the deaths followed Iranian attacks on the country, with the two Italians killed by falling missile debris. Two others were also injured.

In response, a spokesperson for Iran's military told state media that no offensive or military operations had been carried out in the area and that "these accusations should be viewed as a cover for continued malevolence and an attempt to exacerbate insecurity in the region" by U.S. and Israeli forces.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said on Telegram that he had spoken with his Italian counterpart, Antonio Tajani, and expressed regret over their deaths, while stressing that Tehran had no role or involvement in the incident.

"The incident is suspicious and looks like a precooked recipe for war," Araghchi wrote on X.

He also said Iran was ready to cooperate with the governments of Kuwait and Italy in investigating the incident.

Separately, Israel said Thursday that it was prepared to carry out strikes against Iran "at any moment," even without U.S. forces joining the effort, according to Israeli military spokesman Effie Defrin on Israeli broadcaster Kan.

The comments came after U.S. envoy Jared Kushner told a podcast Wednesday that Israel "needs to get out of the constant wartime mindset."

Israel has been pushing the Trump administration to back another offensive against Tehran, with Reuters reporting on Nov. 18 that Israeli spy chief Roman Gofman pushed for joint strikes when he met U.S. officials earlier in the month.

Late Thursday, President Donald Trump said on PBS that the U.S. had "victory at hand" in the Iran conflict and reiterated that victory would come "whenever I want it to be, to be honest."

Speaking during a Thanksgiving call with U.S. military personnel, Trump said Washington was still trying to give Iran "every chance to make a deal and save a lot of lives."

But he added that he was not sure whether Tehran would agree.

"Some people question why. Are they crazy? Maybe they are," Trump said.

#2812. It’s not just you: Durable goods are getting less reliable, from sputtering cars to glitching smart TVs

"They just don't make things like they used to." The phrase, formerly exclusive to grandparents, is now used by consumers of all ages.

Across the United States, durable goods like cars, appliances and electronics are becoming less reliable. In the automobile industry, owners of 3-year-old models reported an average of 204 problems per 100 vehicles as of Feb. 12, the highest level since 2022, according to a report from consumer intelligence company J.D. Power. Reliability is a growing concern for dishwashers and front-load washers too, respectively experiencing an average of 92 and 91 problems per 100 units as of Oct. 29, according to a similar J.D. Power report.

Consumers have noticed: Shoppers dealing with high inflation rates and added tariffs on imported goods are unsatisfied with all kinds of products, from cars and tech devices to furniture and kitchen gear. Just over three-quarters of Americans say they believe companies often sell lower-quality products and services at higher prices, according to an Axios and Harris Poll survey published in May 2025.

To be sure, reliability and durability aren't the only ways to define a product's quality. Aesthetics and performance matter to consumers, too. Your television today may not last as long as the one you grew up with, but it's probably larger and thinner with better picture definition — and it likely costs much less, when adjusted for inflation, says Justin Sirotin, an industrial design professor at the Rhode Island School of Design. Indeed, the average price of a television was roughly $622 in 1996 adjusted for inflation, whereas a 55-inch LCD model now costs $232 on average.

But because they're penny-pinched, consumers are becoming more focused on finding high-value products — ones that perform consistently and reliably — than on price, according to a 2025 Deloitte report.

Unfortunately for shoppers, some businesses are also economically squeezed, and may manufacture products with cheaper, less durable parts to keep their profits growing, Sirotin says. Plus, we've all gotten used to sleek products with impressive features at affordable prices, and when businesses have to deliver on those aspects to grow demand, longevity can drop down the list of priorities. Manufacturers may prioritize quality on the external parts of a product — like the picture quality on a television — and get their cost savings with cheaper internal components that you won't initially notice, says Keyana Sapp, an investigative journalist and creator of the Worse on Purpose newsletter.

In some cases, a product's durability may even be part of a "planned obsolescence," wherein businesses intentionally give goods a certain lifespan so customers need to buy replacements within a predictable timeframe. Planned obsolescence isn't necessarily rampant, and it's hard to prove that any individual company purposefully designs products to fail, Sapp says.

But historically speaking, it's not entirely a conspiracy theory. In 1924, major light bulb manufacturers formed an international coalition and agreed to lower and standardize the lifespan of incandescent light bulbs to 1,000 hours. The cartel broke up during World War II.

You can, of course, try extending the longevity of products you've already bought by paying to repair them, rather than getting a brand-new replacement. But as devices like vacuum cleaners and headphones evolve technologically, they require increasingly specialized tools and labor, says Shahram Mokhtari, lead teardown technician at online repair community and e-commerce site iFixit. Specialized screws, glued components and limited access to repair information can make some products difficult or impossible to fix, the Federal Trade Commission reported in May 2021.

Europe has right-to-repair regulations that require manufacturers of certain products to provide access to replacement parts, tools and information. Only nine U.S. states have similar legislation for consumer electronics.

If you want to know how long a product might last before you buy it, look up its reviews or do some research to understand if it's complicated to fix, Mokhtari recommends. Or, if you have the budget, you can prioritize higher-priced products that might bring both initial quality and long-term longevity. This may require intentional long-term planning for some consumers — even as online shopping has made impulse buying easier, Sirotin notes.

"It takes a lot of intentionality by the consumer to say, 'OK, well, is this chair really a chair that I want to have for 25 years? Or is this the chair I'm going to buy and sit in for six months, and then I'm just going to get another chair?'" says Sirotin.

#2811. Logansport football uses team discipline to roll past Jeanerette

LOGANSPORT — Most coaches would agree that discipline in sports performance is a key element in winning and that was certainly the case on Dale Morvan Field Friday (Nov. 27) when Logansport met Jeanerette in a LHSAA Non-Select Division IV quarterfinal contest.

Since official statistics weren't available, we aren't sure the exact number of penalties Jeanerette incurred in the 41-18 Logansport victory, but it was in excess of 20. Meanwhile, the Tigers (11-1) move into the semifinals where they'll host Homer next Friday night with a Superdome trip on the line. The Pelicans ousted defending champion Haynesville 14-7 in a slugfest.

Logansport was the antithesis of undisciplined, holding their missteps to about a half-dozen from the Lake Charles-based officiating crew. And Logansport quarterback Kollin Magee made the visitors pay dearly for giving his offense extra chances. He hooked up with Jazavien Barnes three times for bombs that attacked the will of their opponents.

"We stayed to ourselves, did what we were supposed to do and lived to fight another week," Magee said. "They had nothing to lose, and we had a lot on our shoulders right now. We'll keep doing the right things down the road."

Barnes repeatedly managed to get behind the Jeanerette (11-2) defense to haul in TD passes covering 27, 38 and 48 yards to cement his performance in Logansport lore.

"I knew they couldn't guard me, so I just ran right by them," Barnes said. "It's over after that. It feels amazing to move on."

Due to the chippiness between the teams, the coaches elected not to shake hands after the final whistle while a small army of DeSoto Parish deputies took the field. With the win, the Tigers avenged getting eliminated from the 2025 playoffs by Jeanerette. Logansport coach Kevin Magee said his staff stressed remaining composed throughout the playoffs.

"We had a few years where we didn't keep our composure," Magee said. "We weren't as disciplined as we are now – in the penalty areas – in the little things. We worked hard to change the mentality and in big games it pays off."

Kollin Magee said his teammates had a chip on their shoulders from the 36-8 loss at Jeanerette last season.

"But the message in practice all week was 'we are the big dog now.' Last year we had to go to them. They were the higher seed," Magee said. "They had to come to us this year."

#2810. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2809. 'The Traitors' ended with a $246,300 prize—here's who won and what they told us about spending it

When Shane Beatty, a contestant on NBC's "The Traitors: New Blood," deceived his fellow finalists, he walked away with the entire $246,300 prize. But the emotional toll of winning caught him by surprise.

"The second I had to reveal that I was a Traitor, everything hit me at once," Beatty, a 32-year-old ironworker from Staten Island, New York, tells CNBC Make It. "I had built genuine relationships with these people, and knowing that some of them were blindsided by my decision was difficult."

Now that he's won, Beatty has a plan for his money, starting with setting aside a sizable share for Uncle Sam. As for the rest, he's more interested in financial security than the trappings of newfound wealth.

After winning, Beatty doesn't expect his day-to-day life to change much.

"I'm still going to get up, go to work, and continue doing what I've been doing as a Local 580 ironworker," he says. "I love my career, and winning this money doesn't change that."

Beatty plans to pay for a wedding he and his fiancée previously struggled to afford, help his mother pay down debt and clear his own debts before saving the rest.

"What it does give me is an opportunity to take some financial pressure off, be a little more comfortable and put myself in a better position for the future," he says.

But before spending his winnings, Beatty is accounting for what could be his single biggest expense — taxes.

"First thing I'm gonna do with the money is put at least $100,000 in a separate bank account just so I can have it ready for Uncle Sam," he told Entertainment Weekly last week.

Setting aside $100,000 is a reasonable move given Beatty's potential tax liability, according to tax experts.

"Taxes are often an afterthought when winning big on a gameshow," says Riley Adams, a certified public accountant and founder of Sequoia Ridge CPAs. "So, it's definitely smart on his part to get ahead with his tax planning."

Game show winnings are generally taxed as ordinary income, meaning the prize money is added to a winner's other taxable income for the year. That can push a winner into higher tax brackets.

In addition to federal income taxes, Beatty, who lives in New Jersey, will have to account for state income taxes.

While Beatty did not disclose his income to CNBC Make It, ironworkers in the New York City metropolitan area typically earn around $100,000 a year, according to 2025 Bureau of Labor Statistics data.

Assuming Beatty lives in New Jersey, earns $100,000 from his job in New York in 2026, receives his winnings this year, files as a single taxpayer with no dependents and takes the standard deduction, here's how much of his prize money he could keep, according to Adams' calculations:

Under this scenario, Beatty's winnings would push him from the 22% federal income tax bracket into the 35% bracket. Factoring in state income taxes, an estimated 35% of his prize would go to taxes, according to Adams' calculations. Setting aside $100,000 would give Beatty a comfortable cushion under this scenario. His actual tax bill, however, will depend on his income, deductions and other financial circumstances.

Jim Guarino, a certified public accountant and managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts, arrived at a similar estimate.

"Folks tend to get excited by the 'gross amount' of their total winnings," but Beatty is "extremely tax-aware" in recognizing how much less he'll actually get to keep after taxes, he says.

For Beatty, the money won't radically change his life, but it will give him greater financial security.

"Having a savings cushion and knowing I don't have to worry about living paycheck to paycheck or falling behind on bills means so much to me," he says. "I want to be responsible with this opportunity and make sure it gives me greater financial peace of mind."

#2805. ‘I don’t know these days’ – Giants HC John Harbaugh admits referee decisions around one play don’t make sense

New York Giants head coach John Harbaugh wants some honest clarification on what the NFL's referees want when a player goes to catch the ball.

The NFL have seen multiple major instances this season where fans and teams are leaving games frustrated at the decisions made by the referees. While referees have been criticised over a plethora of penalties, one specific factor has shone through in the last week.

There have been three highly controversial plays where the referees have ruled out touchdown catches because of differing opinions on what 'control of the football' means. Giants coach Harbaugh seems to have had enough.

Harbaugh openly admitted in a press interview on November 27 that he simply does not know what a catch is in the NFL, with the replay center making the situation greyer than it needs to be.

"I thought I did. Now it goes to replay, and I don't know. Once it goes to replay, it becomes real grey. It was the same last year, and that hasn't changed."

There are three incidents that have brought this topic to the forefront of NFL discourse right now.

First, in the fourth quarter of the November 22 clash between the Jacksonville Jaguars and Giants, Jags WR Parker Washington had a sensational TD catch that was ruled incomplete, even after a video challenge by the team. This was over Washington 'failing to control the ball.'

Second, Rams WR Konata Mumpfield had a TD catch on November 25 against the Green Bay Packers that was ruled incomplete for failing to control the ball while crashing onto the field.

Finally, and the most high-profile of them all, was the incomplete TD for Kansas City Chiefs WR Rashee Rice in their Thanksgiving loss to the Buffalo Bills. What could have been a game-winner with under two minutes left was ruled out, prompting the Bills to gain control and kick a field goal for a 13-10 win.

Rice's incomplete TD catch led to widespread calls against NFL referees, including from Patrick Mahomes' wife, Brittany Mahomes. But while the ambiguity in what is a catch continues, referees will keep coming under fire for their calls.

#2804. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2801. The Tech Download: Will Jensen Huang’s return be the spark the shaky AI market needs?

The recent Nvidia saga has had more twists and turns than an Agatha Christie novel.

It all started with Nvidia co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's admission that he had been unfaithful to his wife. Days later, amid an independent board investigation and broader scrutiny over national-security concerns, he resigned. Less than two weeks later, Huang was back at the helm.

During all of that, Nvidia stock lost over $1 trillion and then regained just under $1 trillion.

The leadership drama has played out at a time when the artificial intelligence market is looking shakier than it has in some time.

Neoclouds have seen stocks plummet in recent months as investors question the sustainability of the AI boom. Chip stocks like Arm and AMD have both fallen in the past month.

Huang's surprise return saw Nvidia stock surge 31%, with other AI stocks also gaining. But the sheen was off by Tuesday's close, with Nvidia and a host of chip stocks falling.

The sharp reversal raises questions for investors: did Huang's return restore confidence in Nvidia, or did it spark a short-lived relief rally as the market faces broader questions about returns on AI investment?

Judging by the first few days of his return, it's a hard one to call.

Volatility in the tech sector looked set to continue on Friday, as tech stocks rebounded again.

"Wall Street pared its Monday advance as sobriety returned to the market and a focus returned to fundamentals," wrote Kyle Rodda, analyst at Capital.com, in a note at market close on Tuesday.

"While Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO is indubitably a boon for the markets and the overall AI trade, it does not assuage every risk and concern about an industry confronting a potential peak in its investment boom and moderating earnings growth."

The early week rally was "largely driven" by Huang's return, said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares.

"Fresh tariff threats from the Trump administration have added to market uncertainty," she added. "The short-term rally has reversed course, and Nvidia's share price is likely to trade sideways between $125 and $170 in the near term."

Nvidia stock closed at $149.30 on Wednesday, giving it a $3.6 trillion market cap — a figure around $2 trillion lighter than it was at its peak in October.

"We expect a selective recovery across the AI ecosystem, and NVDA to trade sideways and stay volatile below its October peak at least until the Dec 1–3 Congressional appearances," Egor Tolmachev, senior analyst at Freedom Capital Markets, said in a note on Wednesday.

Other key AI stocks have also been volatile. Anthropic's first week as a public company has been mixed. AMD has fallen 25% in the past five days, and memory chip maker Micron has dropped 18%. Both spiked on Monday before paring gains.

The Huang evangelists might've been hoping for a speedier return to AI boom times.

But while the Nvidia CEO's return has removed one source of uncertainty around the chip giant, the volatility among AI stocks suggests investors are still trading on questions much bigger than who occupies the chief executive's office.

Tech German software major SAP is reportedly weighing cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push.

U.S. adds Megaspeed to trade blacklist over export control concerns amid Nvidia controversy.

Robots could be doing household chores in just 1-2 years, a Chinese EV firm says.

SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts.

Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan, according to people inside the room.

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's AI vision while also defending himself in Washington, my colleague Kif Leswing wrote on Tuesday.

If that's the case, it's just fine with him.

Speaking to MMAjunkie.com, Song said that he's willing to fight Dvalishvili next to prove he's worthy of a UFC bantamweight title shot.

"They haven't told me yet – I am waiting. But I am OK to fight one more time. I will get another knockout, then fight for my belt. … Yes, I think Merab makes sense. He is asking for the fight, and I will take it. We will fight, then I will get title shot," Song said.

For what it's worth, Dvalishvili has also said that he wants to fight Song next, so it appears that both men want this fight, and now it's up to the UFC to book it.

If a Song vs. Dvalishvili fight does happen, then it could very well headline a UFC Fight Night card given the potential title implications, or it could be on the main card of a numbered UFC event since it's a big fight between two of the best fighters in the weight class.

While a rematch between Yan and O'Malley hasn't been made official yet, it does feel like that's the direction that the UFC is leaning, and if that happens, then Song believes that the champ will retain his belt.

"If they fight again, O'Malley will probably win again if the judges give it to him. I think Petr Yan is the more skilled fighter between them," Song said.

If Yan vs. O'Malley 2 gets finalized, look for it to headline a numbered UFC event next year.

#2799. Spain’s election could pull Madrid closer to Trump’s Washington

Spanish voters head to the polls on Sunday for a snap election that could bring down socialist Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who has repeatedly clashed with U.S. President Donald Trump.

It could also put the far right in government for the first time in the country's modern democratic era.

The center-right Popular Party is the frontrunner, and it could win a majority with far-right Vox, according to El País's final polling averages. A government led by the PP could shift Spain's approach to Washington, Beijing and NATO.

It's the first test of whether Europe's mainstream conservatives are willing to bring the far right into government, ahead of elections in France and Poland next year.

Despite Spain's economy growing 2.7% year over year in the third quarter, more than twice the euro zone's 1.3%, the campaign has been dominated by housing and cost-of-living crises.

Inflation soared to 4.9% in the year to October, above a euro zone average of 4%. November's flash estimate published Friday showed Spanish inflation cooling to 4.1%, still well above the 2.3% reading at the start of the year. In an October YouGov survey, 53% of respondents named housing one of the country's two most important issues.

Home purchase prices nearly doubled in the decade to the second quarter of 2026, according to Eurostat. In 2025, house prices rose nearly three times as fast as household incomes, the European Commission said.

Nationwide protests began after an 87-year-old woman died two weeks after being evicted from her Madrid apartment in September.

Sánchez called the snap election after lawmakers rejected two government decrees designed to broaden housing protections, including limiting rent increases and blocking certain evictions and automatic lease renewals. Congress later ratified a revised version of one decree, limiting rent increases and evictions in some circumstances.

Sánchez has made a name for himself internationally after clashing with Trump over NATO spending, the war in Gaza and Spain's refusal to let U.S. forces use its bases for operations against Iran. Trump even threatened to cut off "all trade" with Spain during the dispute over the bases.

At a Nov. 21 rally in Tenerife, Sánchez asked: "What good has President Trump brought to the United States and, by extension, to the world?"

He has also told voters that the PP and Vox would roll back government cost-of-living assistance. Days before the election, Sánchez's government approved 1.21 billion euros ($1.33 billion) to extend public transport discounts through 2027 and approved a general pay increase of up to 4.5% for 2027.

But in a final pre-election survey for El Español on Nov. 23, 66% of respondents gave Sánchez a failing grade and 29.5% a passing one. His government has faced scrutiny over its handling of the Ceuta migrant crisis, two deadly train collisions this year and corruption cases involving people close to the prime minister.

PP leader Alberto Núñez Feijóo, the current favorite to replace Sánchez, has made alleged government corruption and inflation central to his campaign. At a Nov. 22 rally in Murcia, he said the government wanted voters to forget "that inflation is eating into your pay check, that you can't afford a home, that you're paying more than ever for everything."

The PP led the final El País polling average, published Nov. 24, with 31.4%, followed by Sánchez's Socialists at 29.1% and Vox at 17.9%. Several seat projections put the PP and Vox above the 176 seats needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. If Vox joins a government, it would be the first far-right party to do so since Spain returned to democracy after Francisco Franco, the dictator who ruled for 40 years, died in 1975.

The PP wants to repeal the 2023 housing law and scrap rent caps in designated high-pressure areas. It proposes speeding up construction permits and lowering taxes on first homes for buyers under 40. Vox also wants to repeal the housing law and would prioritize Spanish citizens for subsidized housing and public services.

Sánchez's Socialists want to extend renter protections and make at least a quarter of new homes public or permanently price-restricted. It would use proceeds from a bank levy to support housing, which it says will raise a further $1.5 billion. The PP has pledged to scrap this.

The PP also favors easing climate rules, extending the life of nuclear plants and replacing a decree that sets tight new renewable-power requirements on data centers with large new grid connections.

A PP-Vox coalition would have its tensions, with immigration likely to be a major pressure point as Vox has an increasingly hard line on deportation and the militarization of some borders. The parties clashed in regional governments over the distribution of unaccompanied migrant children, leading Vox to quit several coalitions in 2024.

Immigration has since gained more prominence amid the government's handling of reports that more than 70,000 people crossed from Morocco into the Spanish enclave of Ceuta on July 30 and 31.

"Immigration has played a significant role in supporting Spain's recent growth, a strong role for Vox could complicate the government's ability to sustain that," said Jess Middleton, senior Europe analyst at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

The parties also differ on defense commitments: both support more investment, but neither has committed to NATO's target of spending 5% of GDP on defense. The PP pledges closer NATO cooperation, while Vox puts greater emphasis on border security.

The PP favors closer integration with the European Union, while Vox wants more independence from it. The success or failure of a PP-Vox partnership will also have wider lessons for Europe "where long-standing firewalls against far-right parties are coming under strain," added Middleton, pointing to the rise of Germany's AfD and the prospect of Marine Le Pen potentially winning next year's election in France.

A shift to the right could improve relations between the U.S. and Spain. Sánchez used his tenure to strengthen ties with Beijing, the country's largest goods trading partner outside the EU.

The U.S. remained Spain's largest source of foreign direct investment. Flows more than doubled to 11.6 billion euros in the 12 months through June 2026, from 5.1 billion euros in the previous 12 months, according to Spain's Economy Ministry.

Carla Hobbs, head of the European Council on Foreign Relations' Madrid office, expects a PP-led government to seek closer ties with Washington and bring its China policy more in line with the EU's. Vox could complicate that. The first test for Washington would be whether a new government changes Spain's position on defense spending or the use of its bases.

#2797. EU leaders fail to break $1.7 trillion budget deadlock as political pressure mounts

European leaders failed to break a deadlock over the EU's next long-term budget on Thursday, leaving negotiators under pressure to meet their year-end target before a busy 2027 election cycle adds further complications.

National leaders are divided over the European Union's next long-term budget, which sets spending priorities and limits from 2028 to 2034. It requires unanimous approval from all 27 member states to pass, as well as a majority vote in favor from members of the European Parliament.

Representatives from EU member states met on Wednesday and Thursday to discuss the latest budget proposal from European Council President António Costa, which amounts to a spending plan worth 1.58 trillion euros ($1.73 trillion). It marks a downward revision of around 10% from the original proposals for the bloc's long-term budget — known as the Multiannual Financial Framework, or MFF.

The EU's budget negotiations come against a backdrop of surging government borrowing costs, spiraling inflation and sluggish growth across much of the continent, as well as concerns about rising budget deficits in various EU countries.

Failure to meet the year-end deadline for the budget to be agreed risks deepening the political deadlock at the center of the ongoing talks.

At a summit in Brussels Thursday, budget talks went on for 12 hours, according to a BBC report — with some participants said to have walked out of the meeting as conflicts emerged around spending priorities and aid for Ukraine.

A spokesperson for the European Commission was not available to comment on Thursday's talks.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz told reporters on Thursday evening that the talks had brought "new movement" towards reducing the overall size of the budget, adding that leaders would reconvene in mid-December to resume talks.

But he also said: "The European Union comprises of 27 member states, not 23 or 24."

Merz, alongside the leaders of Denmark, the Netherlands, Austria, Finland and Sweden, said in September that the original 2-trillion-euro proposal would need to be reduced by "several hundred billion euros."

"We must make choices," they said in a letter addressed to Costa and Irish Taoiseach Micheál Martin, pointing out that their six countries finance almost 40% of all contributions to the budget.

They called for funding to be focused on defense spending, competitiveness and innovation and "the fight against irregular migration."

Costa labeled the revised budget plans "realistic" when he presented them last week, noting that despite the 10.2% reduction in spending the proposals still represented a 120% jump in competitiveness funding and a 232% upswing for defense compared to the current budget.

"We didn't reach an agreement," he said in a statement after Thursday's meeting. "Regarding the global volume of the budget, some member states asked for further reductions, most opposed further cuts. And some even proposed to reverse some cuts."

Some European parliamentarians also appeared unconvinced by the new proposals.

"Parliament is consistent and proposes more investments, [the] Council is inconsistent and proposes cuts," Romanian MEP Siegfried Mureşan said on Wednesday.

"When the Council finally reaches a position, that's when negotiations will start, not when they end. As prime ministers cannot dictate to national parliaments, prime ministers also cannot dictate to the European Parliament to adopt a budget that is too small, and which does not equip Europe for the future."

Elections are set to go ahead in key EU economies including Spain, Poland and Italy over the next 12 months, while France's spring presidential election is at the forefront of regional leaders' minds.

Current front-runner for the French presidency, far-right candidate Marine Le Pen, has previously called for the country to leave the EU and to cut its contributions to the bloc's budget.

Incumbent French President Emmanuel Macron stressed on Thursday that he had limited time left to have his say on EU spending.

"After 31 December, I will no longer be in a position, politically or democratically, to reopen discussions on the budget and bring them to a conclusion, as we will be ​entering a period far too close to the presidential election in France," he told reporters after the talks, according to French publication France 24.

France, the EU's second-biggest economy after Germany, is being tasked with drastically cutting spending to bring its 5.1% national deficit closer to an EU guideline of 3%, while reeling from months of mass protests, riots and strikes over education funding and a cost-of-living crisis.

#2796. Why Pearl-Cohn's return to TSSAA football state championship was never in doubt

LEXINGTON — The thought crossed Marshall commit Lance Donaldson's mind that his Pearl-Cohn football career could be over.

With two weeks left in the regular season, the Firebirds' leading rusher suffered an ankle injury against White House on Oct. 23, forcing him to miss the final week of the regular season and potentially more.

He said the injury was "a blessing in disguise," letting him add an extra element to his game and be a better player for his team.

Donaldson logged 54 yards and two touchdowns on 13 carries giving Pearl-Cohn an edge in a 42-16 victory against Lexington (13-1) in TSSAA football playoff semifinals, in Class 4A on Nov. 27.

More TSSAA football championship game pairings for 2026 BlueCross Bowl matchups

The win pushes Pearl-Cohn (11-3) back into the 4A state championship game where it will face Alcoa (14-0) at 10 a.m. CT on Dec. 5 at Finley Stadium in Chattanooga. Its the second straight trip to the BlueCross Bowl for Pearl-Cohn and the program's eighth overall championship appearance, having won titles in 1996, 1997 and 2023.

"He's (Donaldson) been playing lights out on both sides of the ball," said Pearl-Cohn coach Tony Brunetti, who will coach in his sixth title game on Dec. 5. "That's why he's such a powerful kid and is signing with Marshall next week."

Part of Donaldson's deep dive into his game was getting to know those around him. He wanted to get closer to his linemen, not just as teammates, but really understanding the details of what they were doing in front of him.

"I feel like my game has gotten better (since the injury)," Donaldson said. "I was able to look at my game from a mental standpoint and look at the things I could do better. Now, I'm able to attack those points and strengthen the ones I'm already good at.

Since returning from injury, Donaldson has contributed six touchdowns in four games. Prior to that, he had just four scores in nine games.

"He (Donaldson) does everything the right way," Brunetti said. "You don't see him talking or anything. He's a hard-nosed player. He's even lifting weights before the game.

Donaldson is hoping for a full-circle moment against Alcoa in Chattanooga. Like many Pearl-Cohn seniors, his career started off with a state championship in 2023. He wants to leave the same way he came in. His Firebirds took care of business, handing Lexington (13-1) its only loss this season.

It'll be a tall task for Pearl-Cohn as Alcoa has won 11 consecutive state titles.

"It's great to get one coming in and to get another going out is going to be an amazing feeling," Donaldson said.

#2795. SpaceX shares jump over 4% amid tech rally and after Musk says he will expand on Trump Account share donations

SpaceX shares rose 4.83% in Friday's shortened trading session, amid broader Nasdaq gains and after CEO Elon Musk expanded his SpaceX share-donation plan for U.S. children.

Other tech-related stocks, including Micron, Qualcomm, Lumentum and Tesla all gained over 5% on Friday as the sector rebounded from a heavy sell-off earlier this week. SanDisk was Friday's biggest mover as investors rotated back into some AI trade names, surging 15.96%.

Musk posted on X on Thursday, "I will be donating another share of SpaceX to all kids in America every year."

After Musk's post, SpaceX shares jumped more than 7% in overnight trading on Blue Ocean ATS, an alternative trading system for U.S. equities that runs during the nighttime hours. U.S. stock exchanges were closed Thursday for the Thanksgiving holiday and closed at 1 p.m. ET on Friday.

Shares closed 1.30% higher on Wednesday ahead of Thanksgiving.

The latest pledge follows SpaceX's announcement Wednesday that it would donate one share of its Class A stock to all active Trump Accounts, tax-advantaged investment accounts for American children, with Musk personally matching the gift.

In a separate post on X, Musk said SpaceX's contribution would be transferred next month, while his personal donation would follow when his lockup period expires in June next year.

Nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created as of October, according to the White House.

Since the program's launch in July, families and friends have contributed more than $1 billion to the accounts, bringing total deposits from all sources to more than $9 billion, CNBC reported earlier this week.

The FCC also announced Wednesday that it granted SpaceX approval to deploy 1 million data center satellites for the company's Starmind constellation.

Speaking on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Friday, FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said the authorization of SpaceX's satellites will have ripple effects within the industry as well.

"Not just SpaceX, but all sorts of different players in this market. Because at the end of the day, we want everyone to compete and succeed. I think we're going to see a lot of players entering this space soon," Carr said.

#2794. Chinese chip stocks notch biggest ever one-day gains as Beijing signals broader market support

Chinese stocks rebounded sharply on Friday after a rare intraday state media readout cheered investors as top policymakers pledged to expand the role of state-backed funds in supporting the market.

The STAR index surged 14%, led by Chinese chipmakers such as CXMT, Cambricon and SMIC. The CSI Semiconductor index of 40 chip stocks climbed by 17.6% in its biggest single-day gain on record, surpassing the prior record of nearly 17% in late 2024, according to Wind Information.

The gains were widespread across mainland and Hong Kong stock indexes. The Hang Seng index rose 2%, while the CSI 300 index climbed 4%, its biggest jump since October 2024, according to Wind data.

The rally comes as governments pour support into domestic chip industries while tightening controls on sensitive technology in the global race for artificial intelligence.

The U.S. unveiled a new rule last week to further restrict Chinese companies’ access to advanced AI chips, including through overseas data centers, adding pressure on Beijing to accelerate its push for semiconductor self-sufficiency.

Friday's rally comes after the CSI 300 on Thursday sank to its lowest level since the escalation of the U.S.-China tariff war in the spring of 2025, according to Wind data.

In recent months, investors have weighed the commercial feasibility of Chinese artificial intelligence developers and tighter U.S. restrictions on advanced chips.

Chinese Premier Li Qiang oversaw a meeting Friday morning of the State Council, the country's top governing body, which saw officials pledge to strengthen the role of "National Team" — referring to state-backed funds — to stabilize markets and ensure ample liquidity, according to a readout released by state media Xinhua News.

The explicit reference to the "national team" is a first for a State Council meeting, said Bob Chen, a Shanghai-based venture capitalist and economist.

Authorities also stressed that "capital markets are crucial to the broader economy," according to the readout, a rare comment from a government that has not always looked favorably on the financial sector.

"Stock market performance is now prioritized at the highest level for the Chinese government," which is boosting investor confidence, Chen said.

"That's very strong language from the top officials," said Zong Ke, a portfolio manager at a Shanghai-based quantitative fund.

By Ke's calculations, stock trading volumes surged rapidly after the readout was released around 1:00 p.m. local time (12:00 a.m. ET). Full-day turnover across the Shanghai, Shenzhen and Beijing exchanges was 52% higher than the previous session.

Policymakers also vowed to strengthen the role of "stabilization funds" like Central Huijin Investment, a state-owned investment firm that holds equity stakes on behalf of the State Council.

That message "effectively institutionalized Central Huijin's role as a state stabilization fund," said Hong Hao, managing partner of Lotus Asset Management. "And it's significantly positive news for the market."

Hao expects this market rally to prove more durable than the "fleeting bounce" triggered by Beijing's stimulus talks in the past, which wore off in days. "This time it's for real."

The readout of the Cabinet's meeting, typically released well after trading hours, was published around noon local time during trading hours, an unusual timing that's viewed by markets as a bullish signal.

The State Council also pledged better policies to steer medium- or long-term funds, such as insurance and social security funds, into equities, and encouraged listed companies to pay more dividends and step up share buybacks, while vowing a crackdown on financial fraud to protect investors.

The national team will accelerate asset purchases, Ke said, adding that he expects more supportive policies to follow, such as further loosening the institutional constraints that have limited long-term funds from investing in stocks.

Beijing's reliance on intervention to offset stock volatility has, however, raised questions about the market's ability to sustain longer-term gains.

But the market needs this kind of support right now, said Ke, as the near-term aim is to ease liquidity pressure and restore confidence so the market doesn't fall much further.

Chinese AI-related stocks also carry less downside risk than their U.S. counterparts, as the companies have spent less on AI so far and their shares have already been through a sharp correction, Ke said.

That, together with the pledged state backing, has given investors more confidence that the market may be nearing its bottom, Ke added.

#2793. UNC vs. West Virginia Live Discussion

The Tar Heels are in Charlotte to face off against West Virginia as part of the Dick Vitale Invitational. The Heels are going into this one with just one loss on the season, while the Mountaineers have two losses, both of which were against ranked teams. This will likely be the Heels' toughest test so far, but this is also a bit of a different team from the one that played Georgia. Having Cameron Fens in the lineup has made a difference, and one would argue that Terrence Brown playing point guard in Neo Avdalis' absence has had more of a positive impact on the team than a negative. The thing, though, is his ability to be a facilitator will be tested big in a big way tonight.

The Mountaineers are currently averaging 8 steals per game, which is slightly higher than what Carolina has been doing. Having a team that is that active on defense when you do not have your starting point guard can be tough, so we'll see if Brown is able to take care of the ball. On the flip side, West Virginia is also averaging 9.8 turnovers per game, which funny enough is also somewhat similar to UNC's numbers. This game will ultimately come down to who can take care of the ball better and who will be able to rebound, but of course neither matter if the ball isn't going into the basket. Given how aggressive the Heels have been in the paint — mostly because of the crazy stuff Brown has been able to do — it's hard to see scoring being the big issue tonight.

#2791. New car prices are at a record high. Here's where to look for deals

Consumers who are in the market for a new car may find the latest pricing data somewhat sobering.

The average transaction price for a new vehicle reached an all-time high of $51,109 in October, up 2.1% from a year earlier, according to a report from Kelley Blue Book, an auto valuation company. The amount excludes incentives, taxes and fees.

But for consumers who can't spend that much — or don't want to — the good news is that plenty of buyers are shelling out much less for a vehicle.

"The average is telling us as much about product mix as it is about affordability," Erin Keating, executive analyst at Cox Automotive, which owns Kelley Blue Book, said in the report.

Of the five highest-volume car types sold in October, three — compact and subcompact SUVs, and compact cars — sold at prices far below the overall average transaction price, she said. For example, the average amount paid for a compact car in October was $28,030.

Additionally, sales of vehicles priced from about $28,000 to $38,000 climbed 9.6% from a year earlier.

"Consumers are still buying plenty of vehicles well below the headline average transaction price, even as strong demand for full-size pickups and other larger, well-equipped vehicles keeps pushing the average to new highs," Keating said.

The record price comes amid a 34% jump in the average cost of a new vehicle since October 2019, when it was $38,259, according to Kelley Blue Book data.

While pandemic-related supply shortages helped drive up prices earlier this decade, consumers' appetite for larger, more expensive vehicles like SUVs and pickup trucks has also pushed the average higher. Full-size pickups, for instance, sold for an average of $67,912 in October, according to Kelley Blue Book.

Yet buyers who would struggle to afford a $50,000-plus new car can still spend less and find deals in the current market.

For starters, automakers have been slower than usual to roll out 2027 models, at least in part because 2026 cars continue to sell well at current prices, said Ivan Drury, director of insights at Edmunds, an automotive research company. In October, 76% of new-vehicle sales were for current-year models, compared with 59% a year earlier.

This slowdown in the annual model-year transition means more 2027 cars will hit dealer lots soon, which suggests dealers and automakers are motivated to clear 2026 inventory, Drury said.

But waiting until the last week of the year to buy — thinking dealerships are desperate to make the sale — won't necessarily get you a better deal, he said.

"You're not going to get massive savings by waiting," Drury said. "Everybody's busiest time of the year is between Christmas and New Year's, but it's not like the best deals are then — that's a perception thing."

Some manufacturers are offering incentives on certain 2026 models, which can reduce the cost of a new car by thousands of dollars.

For example, the 2026 Nissan Pathfinder, a midsize SUV with a starting price of $41,565 — which includes destination charges — comes with a rebate of $5,000 for qualified buyers through Nov. 30, according to Cars.com, an online automotive marketplace.

Just be sure to check your local dealerships to confirm participation for any manufacturer's incentives, experts say.

Look for cars that have been sitting on dealer lots for an extended time, Drury said. In September, new cars took 58 days to sell, on average, according to Edmunds.

Generally, dealerships consider cars unsold for 60 to 90 days to be aging inventory, which may motivate them to offer a better deal, according to Autotrader.com, an online auto marketplace owned by Cox Automotive.

"You'll find the vehicles that dealers definitely want to get rid of the most," Drury said. Looking for older inventory will be "your quickest way to find a dealership that's thinking, 'Yes, I too want to get rid of this. Help me help you,'" he said.

Listings on some car-shopping websites include how long a car has been on a dealership's lot. You can also ask the dealership how long a particular car has been in its inventory.

Leasing may present an opportunity to get into a car with lower monthly payments than financing the same vehicle, depending on the terms of the lease.

However, fewer attractive leasing deals are available, a trend that started during the pandemic, Drury said. Plus, leases, which are typically for three years, usually come with mileage limits and charges for excessive wear and tear.

Nevertheless, leasing may make financial sense for a consumer who "gets that two- or three-year itch for a new car," Drury said.

This is because after the first two or three years of making payments on a car loan — a time period when the steepest depreciation generally occurs — the car's value may be less than what is still owed on the loan, depending on the specific terms. This is known as being upside down or underwater, or having negative equity. In that case, the amount must either be paid off or added to the buyer's new loan.

"We see so many cars traded in that are 3 years old, and the customer is upside down by $7,000, $8,000 or $10,000 and they roll that into another loan," Drury said.

You also may be able to find a good deal on a used car that's not too old.

The average transaction price for a 3-year-old vehicle was $32,039 in the third quarter, down slightly from $32,461 in the second quarter but 3.1% higher than $31,067 a year earlier, according to Edmunds. It's also higher than the average $27,133 list price for used cars of any age in October, according to Cox.

However, these 3-year-old cars are selling for far less than they did when they were new — $16,562 below the original sticker price of $48,601, on average. That marks the largest dollar gap Edmunds has on record, surpassing the previous high of $16,252 in the last quarter of 2019.

Some 3-year-old models tend to take longer to sell than others, which may give buyers some negotiating power, according to Edmunds. For example, a 3-year-old Jeep Cherokee, with an average transaction price of $23,138 — compared with its original price of $42,263 — takes an average of 64 days to sell, according to Edmunds.

Additionally, a good credit score can help keep the cost of financing a car purchase down. The higher your score, the lower the interest rate you can get, generally speaking.

And, finally, if you're trading in a car, be sure to shop it around, Drury said.

"Get multiple offers. They can vary by thousands of dollars," Drury said. "Have those offers in hand when you're at the dealership that has the next car you want to buy."
