#2790. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2789. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2785. Budget airline Norse Atlantic files for bankruptcy in Europe after failing to secure funding

Budget carrier Norse Atlantic filed for bankruptcy Friday, saying it failed to secure funding after soaring fuel costs strained the company's operations throughout the year.

Its parent company, Norse Atlantic ASA, concluded that the Norwegian carrier no longer has a basis to continue operations.

"Despite extensive efforts, the Company has so far not been able to secure sufficient funding to meet its short-term liquidity needs," the firm said in a statement Friday.

It comes days after Norse said it's facing a "critical financial situation" and was considering its options from a strategic takeover to ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing. The company's shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

The board has now filed a bankruptcy petition with the Agder District Court, where a trustee will be appointed to manage the process further.

"The Board of Norse Atlantic Airways AS has made the difficult decision to file for bankruptcy despite extensive efforts from the Company and the Group to find a solution," Norse Atlantic CEO Eivind Roald said.

"We are deeply sorry for the impact this has on our passengers, employees and partners. The Board, management and advisers of the listed entity Norse Atlantic ASA and the UK subsidiary are working to find a solution for the remaining part of the business to continue operations," Roald added.

The company has canceled all flights, and ticket sales have been discontinued.

Norse was founded in March 2021 and offered cheap flights between Europe, North America, Africa and Asia.

The Iran war began on Feb. 28 and caused jet fuel prices to skyrocket, forcing airlines around the world to grapple with higher costs. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse Atlantic's Roald told CNBC in May that he wasn't "overly concerned" about access to fuel but flagged that the company had canceled over 700 flights, which impacted more than 240,000 passengers.

Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary warned in April that if jet fuel prices continued to stay elevated throughout the year, then other European airlines were likely to face "financial difficulties" during the winter season.

In fact, several European airlines have stopped operating this winter, including Slovakia's AirExplore, Spain's AlbaStar, Ireland's Fly4 and Spain's Plus Ultra, which has canceled all flights but hasn't permanently closed.

#2783. Africa’s richest man is taking his mega-refinery public in a blockbuster IPO

One of the world's largest oil refineries is set to become Africa's biggest public firm in a highly anticipated offering that the continent's richest man has marketed as an "IPO for the people."

Dangote Petroleum Refinery and Petrochemicals FZE, founded by Nigerian billionaire Aliko Dangote, is expected to begin trading in Lagos on Dec. 1, according to multiple media reports, following regulatory approval. A Dangote Refinery spokesperson did not respond to a CNBC request for comment.

Reuters' calculations put Dangote Refinery's IPO-implied valuation at about $47 billion, based on the offer price and the company's registered share count. That would make it the biggest stock market listing Africa has ever seen.

The Lagos-based refinery is regarded as a crown jewel of Nigeria's energy industry given the facility's role in transforming the oil-rich country's fuel market since it started operating in 2024.

"With a minimum subscription of just 10 shares, the offer is designed to give ordinary Nigerians and Africans an opportunity to own a stake in the refinery," Aliko Dangote said at the IPO launch in early September.

"The IPO is positioned not simply as a capital-raising exercise, but as an opportunity for workers, entrepreneurs and everyday Nigerians to participate in the refinery's future growth," he added.

Built for around $20 billion, the Dangote Refinery is now one of the largest in the world, with a crude oil distillation capacity of between 650,000 to 700,000 barrels per day.

It has emerged as a key provider of jet fuel in recent months, with buyers pivoting to West Africa amid Middle Eastern supply disruptions due to the Iran war.

"In theory, it's a win-win situation," Cyril Widdershoven, a maritime, energy and geopolitical analyst at Blue Water Strategy, told CNBC by phone.

Dangote Refinery has bolstered its reputation as a key Atlantic Basin facility amid soaring global demand for refined petroleum products, Widdershoven said, and the company intends to double its refining capacity to 1.4 million barrels per day by 2029, which could further strengthen its competitiveness in global markets.

However, despite the refinery's importance to Nigeria's economy, questions remain over its investment case, Widdershoven said, citing construction costs, commissioning delays, and the possibility that new capacity comes online with weaker margins.

"Right now it's like Anthropic or Nvidia, you are now buying the hype," Widdershoven said.

The Dangote Refinery has had "an extremely strong year," Mick Strautmann, market analyst at Vortexa, told CNBC by phone.

Between January and October, Vortexa data showed Dangote's seaborne refined product loadings stood at around 400,000 barrels per day, which is roughly 50% more than the same period last year.

More recently, however, the facility has seen refined-product loading jump to a record of 575,000 barrels per day through the first three weeks of November, surpassing an October record of 550,000 barrels per day, Strautmann said.

"It's been able to ramp up quite spectacularly and provide a lot of these products to the market in an extremely high price environment," Strautmann added.

"So, it's been one of the few places that's adding a lot of fuel supply to the Atlantic Basin, and at the same time, you know, to make West Africa less reliant on imports from other regions, particularly Europe for its gasoline supply," he said.

David Omojomolo, an emerging market economist focused on Africa at Capital Economics, said the upcoming Dangote Refinery IPO has captured global attention because the facility is seen as a rare example of an industrial policy success story on the African continent.

"It's been a good news story, and it's been an optimistic one as well," Omojomolo told CNBC.

"I don't want to be a hostage to fortune and say this time is different … but it does feel like a buzz and it's a question as to does it force other, say, billionaire industrialists to put their money where their mouth is in terms of helping the region's industrialisation prospects," he added.

#2782. Capitals Drop Below .500 After Costly Late Goal Against Florida

Washington had a great offseason, bringing in players like Alex Tuch, who currently leads the team in goals (15) and points (20).

However, the Caps' defense has held them back to start, and they haven't been able to build any momentum in November, going 3-7-0 in their last 10 games.

The Capitals are currently allowing the second-most goals per game in the NHL at 3.87, trailing only the Vancouver Canucks.

The penalty kill continues to struggle as well, ranking 21st in the NHL amid the team's defensive and goaltending struggles.

In a competitive Metropolitan Division, the Caps will have to get their defense and offense clicking sooner rather than later, as they continue to fall farther from the top while their cold spell continues.

#2780. In first game without Aaron Rodgers, Steelers throttle Broncos in blowout win

Aaron Rodgers' fiery exit seemed to wake up the Pittsburgh Steelers.

The Steelers had a wild week. They benched Rodgers on a Tuesday after 11 p.m. The next day Rodgers gave an instantly infamous interview on Pat McAfee's ESPN show, blasting Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy, and he was cut shortly after. The entire franchise seemed to be on fire.

Then the Steelers got to the game Friday. It might have been a short-term boost for a team rallying around its new starting quarterback Mason Rudolph, its coach or just each other after a week of controversy, but the Steelers played a tremendous game and blew out the Denver Broncos 27-5.

"I thought our leaders did a tremendous job of bringing the urgency to their [positional player] rooms and it definitely showed up," McCarthy told reporters postgame.

The Broncos were 8-2 coming in and were outmatched from the first minutes of the game. Rudolph played a solid game, completing 9-of-15 passes for 142 yards and a touchdown. The Steelers' defense kept the Broncos out of the end zone. It was an impressive performance.

The Steelers are 7-4 and there's a very good chance they'll head to the playoffs. There are still plenty of questions over McCarthy's ability to lead after Rodgers' diatribe over his habits like skipping walkthrough practices and not watching film (and very few denials of Rodgers' claims), and an on-field issue over whether Rudolph can be good enough to make the Steelers much more than blowout fodder in the postseason.

But for at least one day, the Steelers looked like a brand new team.

DK Metcalf of the Pittsburgh Steelers celebrates with Pat Freiermuth (88) after scoring a touchdown against the Denver Broncos.

The Steelers were supposed to be the team in chaos. They looked far more put together than the Broncos.

Rallying around Rudolph and themselves after a week of controversy, the Steelers had a remarkable first half. A few minutes into the second quarter, after a Broncos punt, the Steelers led 14-0 and had outgained the visiting team 105-8. Even though the Steelers got tackled in the end zone for a safety after that punt, it didn't really slow them down.

"There's a lot going on. Anytime you're going through a situation where time is taken away from winning and your team responds, it is extremely important and gratifying," McCarthy said. "But the reality of this game … this is a player's game."

Late in the first half, Nix continued his awful day. He had already had the ball slip out of his hands for a fumble that was recovered in the end zone by the Steelers, and inside of the two-minute warning he threw a baffling pass. It was hard to tell who Nix was targeting because it went right to cornerback Jalen Ramsey, who returned it 36 yards for a touchdown and a 24-5 lead. There wasn't a Broncos receiver within the vicinity of the pass.

The Broncos were terrible in the first half. They had 58 yards and didn't score a touchdown, while giving up two to the Steelers' defense. But the Steelers had a lot to do with it. They seemed like a team that was excited to put the drama of the week behind and play football.

The Steelers just had to sit on the lead in the second half. The Broncos made that easy by not doing much.

The Broncos couldn't run the ball against a good Steelers front. Nix was really bad. The Broncos' defense played better in the second half, but Pittsburgh's offense seemed to know that the game was in hand and it just had to not screw things up.

It's not like the Rodgers story is going away for the Steelers.

Mason Rudolph had a very solid start in a time of genuine turmoil, but it's the Steelers defense who really deserves its flowers, getting two of Pittsburgh's touchdowns and forcing three turnovers from quarterback Bo Nix.

Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy is all smiles as he leaves the field, with a well-deserved win through the noise.

This Pittsburgh defense has been really, really fun to watch, even as the score holds in the second half.

#2779. Saudi Arabia shifts Vision 2030 spending amid economic and security pressure

Saudi Arabia's economic diversification strategy has moved from "spending and delivery at any cost" to "spending and delivery at the right cost," Economy Minister Faisal Alibrahim told CNBC on Friday.

It comes after the Kingdom scaled back or cut several flagship projects as it endures a sharp economic contraction.

Alibrahim also said Saudi Arabia would "stop at nothing from realizing long-term sustainable stability in the region" amid ongoing Houthi strikes on civilian and energy infrastructure that have killed and injured dozens and repeatedly rocked oil markets.

"The phase we're in today is one where every riyal is deployed with greater precision... it was clear that while we want to diversify or restructure our economy to depend less on government spend, there was a lot of catch-up that needed to be done, and for that, you saw government spend leading the way," Alibrahim told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

"Right now, that is shifting from spending and delivery at any cost to spending and delivering at the right cost. We're moving towards a policy-first approach to many of our policy areas and... objectives. We're also creating a clearer business environment, one that aligns the regulatory environment in the Kingdom with international laws."

That includes the recent announcement of a 15% minimum effective tax rate on multinational profits, which Alibrahim said would "create a clearer environment for the private sector to operate," as the government looks to attract more external investment.

Saudi Arabia has been on a mammoth capital spending spree since it launched its Vision 2030 plan to reduce its dependence on oil revenue in 2016, deploying the resources of its sovereign wealth fund — the Public Investment Fund, or PIF.

But more recently, it has reassessed the cost and commercial prospects of initiatives such as the new economic region NEOM and the futuristic "The Line" city, withdrawn funding from the LIV Golf tournament and halted construction on a planned giant skyscraper in Riyadh. In January, it said it would postpone hosting the 2029 Asian Winter Games, which had been scheduled for Trojena, a planned part of NEOM.

Saudi Arabia is meanwhile grappling with the destabilizing effects of ongoing strikes by Iran-backed Houthi militants in Yemen, which have hit targets including the PIF tower, Riyadh Airport and the country's largest oil refinery. The danger forced the Kingdom to call off its big international investor conference, dubbed "Davos in the Desert," which was due to be attended by some of the biggest figures in global finance in November.

"The human cost of crisis has been real, and so has been the economic cost," Alibrahim told CNBC.

Alibrahim said infrastructure investments had allowed the Kingdom's energy sector to restore production to near pre-crisis levels. It has meanwhile moved its import and export hub from the east to the west of the country via land-based transport due to the airport strikes, which has restored cargo levels.

He added that the attacks highlight the importance of diversification to the Saudi economy.

Real GDP contracted by 3.8% in the third quarter, according to government figures, with oil activities down 20.4% year-on-year partially offset by a 1.9% rise in non-oil activities. Net foreign direct investment inflows dropped 19.5% year-on-year in the second quarter.

The Saudi Ministry of Finance expects an overall contraction of around 2.1% this year and a rebound of 10.9% next year.

Advisory group Oxford Economics forecasts a more negative outlook. It sees the Saudi economy shrinking 3.4% this year and by 4% in 2027, amid escalating attacks on energy infrastructure even as a new minimum wage and continued Vision 2030 spending are expected to support incomes and domestic demand.

Akanksha Samdani, lead economist at Oxford Economics, said Vision 2030 was entering a new phase focused on returns.

"The government is managing fiscal pressures and shifting budgetary spending towards essential services and strategic priorities, while PIF is becoming more selective in its capital allocation and seeking greater private-sector participation," Samdani said, adding that some giga projects are being scaled back or delayed, while investment continues in sectors such as manufacturing, logistics, tourism, mining and technology.

"Our forecasts already assume that some of the Vision 2030 targets will be achieved later than originally planned. But the overarching goal of economic diversification remains unchanged," Samdani told CNBC by email.

Neil Quilliam, managing director of consultancy Azure Strategy, said investors are assessing whether Houthi attacks demonstrate a persistent vulnerability that could increase the cost and complexity of delivering Vision 2030.

"They have repeatedly demonstrated an ability to target important economic infrastructure and expose weaknesses in Saudi air defences," he told CNBC, with the attacks challenging the "assumption that Saudi Arabia has insulated itself from regional instability."

The economic impact of the strikes will be less about physical damage and more about whether they affect confidence in Saudi Arabia as a destination for tourism, entertainment and sports, a key part of Vision 2030 and a source of foreign capital and expatriate talent, he added.

#2778. ‘Something Special’: Champlin Park Finishes Perfect Season with First Prep Bowl Title

MINNEAPOLIS — Champlin Park knew it was a dominant force after the second week of the season. The Rebels defeated St. Michael-Albertville 35-0 in the opener. St. Michael-Albertville then lost only 7-0 to Minnetonka the next week, so the Rebels applied the transitive property and knew they had something special this season.

They proved it to the rest of the state week after week as they continued to blow out Class 6A and Class 5A opponents. Ten of their 13 games this season went into running clock.

They walked into the Class 6A Prep Bowl on Friday at U.S. Bank Stadium with a 12-0 record, and they walked out of the stadium undefeated and winners of the championship with a complete 25-6 win against Minnetonka (10-3). The championship is the first football title for Champlin Park.

"We played really, really well," Champlin Park head coach Nick Keenan said. "We haven't had many close games."

The Rebels led 25-0 until the final two minutes, and they controlled every aspect of the game. They had 284 total yards to Minnetonka's 159, and they held a time of possession advantage of 32:54 to Minnetonka's 15:06. The Skippers reached the red zone only on their final possession.

The Rebels' offense ran the ball and controlled the clock. They had two drives of more than eight minutes in the game.

"We were blitzing hard and filling in the right gaps, it was just down to that last one yard extra push that kept all their drives going," Minnetonka senior linebacker Myles Augustyn said. "Miles Felton is a really hard runner. Their offense was just really good."

On defense, the Rebels stopped the run and forced the Skippers into uncomfortable third and fourth downs, where they could unleash their aggressive defensive line.

"The last couple games, I've been able to get into a groove as a play caller," Minnetonka head coach Mark Esch said. "I didn't have anything that was like, 'Hey, we can come back to this all the time consistently and move the ball either in the run game or the pass game.'"

Champlin Park senior quarterback Miles Felton was the engine of the Rebels' offense. The North Dakota commit finished with 158 rushing yards, two rushing touchdowns and one passing touchdown.

He combined with senior running back Preston Nelson to bring the Champlin Park offense back to full strength. Felton missed part of the season with a right wrist injury, and Nelson dislocated his right elbow only two weeks ago, but Nelson returned for the Prep Bowl and finished with 106 rushing yards.

In their absence, many Rebels played well and kept the undefeated season alive.

"We have so much depth," Nelson said. "We felt confident, even with guys out."

The Rebels were confident enough to print championship T-shirts to hand out when they won. The coaches and fans also wore neon-orange shirts in honor of Max Akerson, a former Champlin Park student-athlete who died of cancer two years ago. Akerson served as an inspiration to the team during his battle with cancer as he continued to participate in workouts and practices.

"We have so much left in us, and he showed us that," Nelson said.

#2777. The S&P 500 is in a correction. Bear markets and recessions don't always follow

The S&P 500 fell into a correction on Nov. 20 for the first time since April 2025 as stocks pulled back sharply from October's record highs.

The market correction, defined as a 10% decline from a recent high, came as investor concern mounted over a potential slowdown in AI spending, mega-cap tech valuations and the path of Federal Reserve interest rate policy. The S&P 500 fell 3.7% on Nov. 20 alone, closing at 6,910.39, its seventh consecutive daily decline. That left the index 11.6% below the Oct. 6 closing high of 7,818.93.

A correction can deepen into a bear market, defined as a drop of 20% or more from the most recent top. But history shows that even a bear market, let alone a correction, doesn't automatically mean the economy is destined for a recession.

In a CNBC analysis, eight of the 13 S&P 500 bear markets overlapped with recessions, as defined by the National Bureau of Economic Research, and five did not. The NBER says a recession is "a significant decline in economic activity that is spread across the economy and lasts more than a few months."

The 1973-74 oil shock, the dot-com bust in 2000, the 2007-09 financial crisis and the 2020 pandemic downturn all overlapped with recessions. But some major bear markets also occurred without one, including in 1987 and 2022.

Deutsche Bank macro strategist Henry Allen said that every S&P 500 bear market since the 1962 "Kennedy Slide" has coincided with at least one of three factors: a recession, worsening economic data raising recession fears, or a cycle of Federal Reserve rate hikes. None of those are present today, at least so far.

"Much as last week's selloff was severe, it's worth remembering that it has several differences with many of the other big risk-off moves in recent times," the London-based strategist wrote on Tuesday.

Allen said there hasn't been any sudden reassessment of the growth outlook recently, which happened during the Covid-19 shock, when the pandemic pushed the global economy into recession and the S&P 500 plunged 33.9% in just 33 days. Nor is the current situation similar to the 2025 "Liberation Day" tariff period either, which also prompted a reassessment of the growth outlook.

Economic numbers aren't deteriorating either, with the November U.S. flash composite Purchasing Managers' Index, or PMI, at 54.6, above 50 and indicating that business activity continued to expand. Another factor that often coincides with a recession, Allen said, was a big hawkish shift from the central banks, seen in the 1966 and 2022 bear markets.

"Today however, the equity declines have not coincided with a hawkish repricing," despite widespread expectations that the central bank is set to raise rates again at its final meeting of the year, on Dec. 9, Allen said.

Today, markets are pricing fewer Fed rate hikes through September 2027 than they were on Oct. 6, when the S&P 500 set its record, Allen said. "If anything, pricing has shifted slightly dovishly with the equity declines."

Over more than six decades since 1962, bear markets have always coincided with at least one of those three circumstances, according to Allen.

"So it would be unusual to see one today without those" happening too, he said.

#2775. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2774. The Steelers vs Broncos final score has never happened in NFL history

Just a couple weeks ago, the Pittsburgh Steelers and Cincinnati Bengals finished with a final score that had never happened before in NFL history. Now 12 days later, the Steelers are at it again. The 27-5 final is the 1102nd different final score in an NFL game.

When we get a new final score in the NFL, it's called Scorigami. Normally when we get a Scorigami it's because a team scores an absolute boatload of points and often something weird happens, usually with two-point conversions. We got a safety and not much else from the Broncos on Friday, resulting in this Scorigami.

I don't need to tell you this was a strange game with all the defensive scores, but the biggest one for our purposes was the Broncos' safety. It was a pedestrian 14-0 when Jaylen Warren was tackled in the end zone, leading to a 14-2 score. The Broncos scored next on a field goal, to make it 5 points on the board.

A 14-5 final has happened, as has 17-5, and 24-5. It wasn't until the Steelers put the 27 on the board that we got a unique score of 27-5.

If Denver had made their field goal, 27-8 was also a Scorigami.

This is the seventh Scorigami of the 2026 NFL season.

September 13: Chicago Bears 59, Carolina Panthers 37

September 13: Minnesota Vikings 39, Green Bay Packers 22

November 1: Cincinnati Bengals 43, Tennessee Titans 36

November 15: San Francisco 49ers 46, Dallas Cowboys 40

November 15: Cincinnati Bengals 38, Pittsburgh Steelers 18

November 22: Dallas Cowboys 51, Tennessee Titans 30

November 27: Pittsburgh Steelers 27, Denver Broncos 5

#2773. Trump demands probe into 'record setting error' after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. That data is later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five-year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, according to the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting.

#2772. ‘Certainly a Way to Do It,’ ‘Probably Some Strategy Behind It’ — 2-Time Pro Bowler Theorizes on Real Reason for Aaron Rodgers’ ‘Wild’ Mike McCarthy Rant

Aaron Rodgers' fallout with Mike McCarthy ended with the quarterback out of Pittsburgh less than 24 hours after he publicly aired his grievances on "The Pat McAfee Show."

Former Minnesota Vikings running back Robert Smith suspects it may not have been accidental but a well-thought-out strategy from Rodgers.

Speaking with Jim Rome on "The Jim Rome Podcast," the two-time Pro Bowler floated the possibility that Rodgers' explosive appearance on the ESPN show was more than an airing of frustrations. It may also have been a way to secure a quick exit from the Steelers.

Rodgers appeared on McAfee's show Wednesday, a day after being benched in favor of Mason Rudolph. He accused McCarthy of arriving late to meetings, falling asleep, and described an awkward halftime moment against the Philadelphia Eagles when the coach ended a huddle with "One, two, three, Packers."

ESPN reported that multiple league sources corroborated parts of Rodgers' account, while McCarthy also acknowledged the halftime incident.

Smith found the revelations surprising, even if Rodgers' willingness to speak openly was not.

"It's been so wild. But I think, based on all of the conversations, revelations, and openness from Aaron Rodgers throughout this season … it wasn't as surprising that he came out and said something like this. What was more surprising was the content of it. These were just things that you don't hear outside of locker rooms very often," the former Vikings running back said.

IMPROVE YOUR TEAM: PFN's Free Fantasy Trade Calculator

But Smith wondered whether Rodgers had another motive for taking the dispute public.

"I'm guessing there was probably some strategy behind it for Aaron, because if you want to get out, you want to get out fast. Start revealing some of those inside stories about your coach, and you won't be long in the building."

Smith returned to that possibility when Rome asked what Rodgers might have been thinking after losing his starting job.

"Just a guess from me. If he has been benched there, he doesn't want to spend the last part of his career benched. No way."

He then explained how going public could have helped Rodgers avoid waiting for the Steelers to work out another arrangement.

"I don't know if this is true, but game theory would say, if you want to go play somewhere else rather than sitting on a bench, you don't really feel like waiting for them to try and figure out a trade. … You could have asked for the release. But maybe you try and do both," he continued.

"You try and say, 'Hey, it wasn't really my fault, so I'm going to tell these things about him, but I'm also going to do it because I want to get out of the building quickly.' And this is certainly a way to do it. It works."

Rodgers had actually claimed he raised the possibility of a release with McCarthy before the benching became public.

"I left it with him that if you try and bench me or don't change the play call, then you should just release me because I feel like at this point, I'm doing what's right for the organization."

Rodgers cleared waivers on Thursday without being claimed.

Rodgers was graded 67 in PFN's NFL QB Impact Score through his 10 starts this season — the worst season grade of his career.

#2770. Jazz Get Crushed in Fourth Quarter, Lose to Nuggets Late

#2769. SCORIGAMI: The NFL’s list of unique scores has added more in 2026

There had been 1095 unique final scores in NFL history as of the conclusion of the 2025 season. We are now up to 1102 with the ones added during 2026.

With teams scoring more points in games, the extra point moving out to a 33-yard kick, and teams deciding to go for two more often to gain edges, never-before-seen final scores are still happening despite the NFL playing for more than 100 years. These unique scores are also called "Scorigami".

Many of the Scorigami we are seeing are for 40-plus point games that have high scoring from both teams, but we've also been seeing scores in the 30s that have never happened. It's not just high scores that have never happened, though. A game has never finished in a 2-2 tie, for instance. The NFL didn't allow two-point conversions until 1994, and we've never seen an 8-7 game. A game has never ended in a 9-9 tie, among other scores still out there.

Since 1992, there have been three or more Scoigamis in every season. The average since the start of the 2000 season is seven per year with the high of 14 in 2013 and a low of three in 2022 and 2009.

The 2026 NFL season didn't wait to get the Scorigami train rolling. Two NFC North teams won games on the Sunday of opening weekend with scores that had never happened before. They were the 1096th and 1097th unique scores in NFL history.

Normally when we get a Scorigami, it's because something weird happened in the game. This one wasn't that. The Bears just laid the smack down. Chicago scored 14 points in the first, third, and fourth quarters along with a 17-point third quarter. All of those are regular scores, there were just a lot of them. We had a late extra point blocked, but 59-38 also has never happened.

The Packers missed an extra point in the first quarter, but that didn't trigger a whole lot of unique scores on its own. Everything stayed pretty normal until the fourth quarter when Minnesota scored to take the lead. They converted a 2-point conversion to go up 25-22. Scoring 25 points in an NFL game is weird. Then Minnesota added two more 7-point touchdowns on top of it. A 39-point day is pretty rare. (A 32-22 score has also never happened.)

After a two-point conversion made the score a very normal 21-21, things got weird. A Bengals safety made it 23-21. The Bengals couldn't get a two-point conversion on a later touchdown after the Titans scored, making it 29-28. Then the Titans got a two-point on their next score to make it a seven-point lead at 36-29. The Bengals scored and kicked an extra point and the game sat at 36-36 at the end of regulation. It should be noted that an overtime touchdown would have won the game without an extra point before this season and 42-36 has happened before.

The Cowboys didn't get the extra point after scoring a touchdown, which made it 16-10 in the second quarter. In the fourth quarter, the score was 38-19 when the Cowboys got in the end zone and tried to make it an 11-point game but failed at 38-25. That's when things got weirder. Dallas made it 38-32 then the 49ers scored a long touchdown with a 2-point conversion to get the to the very strange 46 points. 46-32 was a Scorigami, as was 46-39, but the Cowboys went for 2 after their last TD and got it to 46-40. Also a Scorigami.

For the second time on the same day, we saw a Scorigami. And it was the Bengals' second in three weeks. With the Bengals up 31-2, the Steelers scored and went for two to make it 31-11. it's weird because it's still a three-touchdown game and doesn't really help him close the gap but whatever. While 31-11 would have been Scorigami, both teams scored again to make it 38-18. The only 38-point Scorigami winners left now are 38-2 and 38-4.

For the second straight week, the Dallas Cowboys were part of a Scorigami and the Titans get on the board for the second time in 2026. Dallas seems to have the formula — score a ton of points. There are a ton of 51-point games that have not hit in the history of the NFL. Down 51-23, the Titans scored with 31 seconds left. 51-23 and 51-29 have both happened before as has 51-31. The Titans kicking the extra point to make is 51-30 was the difference.

The 27 points is normal, but 5 points is not. A safety and a field goal were all the Broncos could muster on Black Friday. A lot of other scores have happened against 5 points, but not 27. When the Steelers added a fourth-quarter field goal for the only points of the second half, it moved from 24-5 (which has happened once before) to 27-5, which has never happened. Scorigami.

8-7: Someone scores a late touchdown and goes for the win, not the tie

9-9: Two struggling offenses end in a 9-9 tie, or a 6-6 game at end of regulation ending this way

25-18: A few ways to get to 18 then a score to win

32-22: A team playing catch up gets to 22 and a team keeping them away gets to 32

41-41: With 40-40 off the board, lets go one more

42-25: A team up late pushes it to a three-possession game

46-17: It's the lowest number against the very common 17

50-3: It's the lowest number against the very common 3

60-14: It's the lowest number against the very common 14

After seven Scorigamis in 2024, there were four NFL games to hit new finals in 2025, two including the high-scoring Buffalo Bills. here is how each of them got there in chronological order:

This game features two high-powered offenses at the height of their powers, but the Bills started going for two in the third quarter and missed on consecutive attempts. Halfway through the fourth quarter, they sat at 25 points, a rare number. A missed extra point by the Ravens put Baltimore at 40 — not an entirely uncommon number on its own — before the Bills' final push. Buffalo made it 40-38 after two touchdowns and an extra point but failed to convert on the game-tying two-point conversion. They got the ball back and kicked a field goal as time expired to win 41-40.

There are several ties still available on this list, but not as many as you would think. The NFL didn't have sudden death overtime until 1974 when they added a 15-minute frame, so there were a bunch of ties prior to 1974 but then a lot fewer. They modified the rule in 2010 so it wasn't sudden death for a first-drive field goal then shortened the time to 10 minutes in 2017. In 2025, they gave every team a possession. With those recent changes, ties can be more common.

Those rule changes directly impacted this game between the Dallas Cowboys and Green Bay Packers. Dallas didn't win after their first-possession field goal because Green Bay was guaranteed a possession. The Packers only had 4:40 on the clock and drove deep into Dallas territory. After shots to the end zone failed, they settled for a 40-40 tie as time expired.

In the third quarter, the Jaguars were up 23-10 and scored another touchdown. Up 19, they knew the Texans had to score three times to win so they tried to make it a 21-point lead to make it three touchdowns to tie or win. That play failed, and they sat at 29 points with a 19-point lead.

The Texans then scored a touchdown plus a two-point conversion to make it an 11-point game. Another touchdown with a failed two-point conversion made it a 5-point game. Houston scored again to go up 30-29 and tried another two-point conversion to ensure a late field goal wouldn't beat them, but that try failed with 31 seconds on the clock.

Here's where things get nuts. A 30-29 score has happened before. But the Texans returned a fumble for a touchdown to make it 36-29 as time expired. A final of 37-29 and 38-29 has happened, but because there was no time on the clock, there was no extra point attempt, and the final stood at Scorigami.

For Buffalo's second Scorigami of the year, they also topped 40 points. The Buccaneers started the shenanigans in the third quarter when they scored a touchdown to make it 26-21. They went for two to try and make it 28-21 but the conversion failed. Buffalo got to 31 the old-fashioned way, with four touchdowns and PATs plus a field goal, and the score was 31-26. After another touchdown, the Bucs tried to make it a three-point lead with another two-point conversion that failed, sticking them at 32 points. Buffalo scored another touchdown to make it 37-32 and failed the two-point conversion to try and make it a seven-point game. The Bills' final touchdown game them a 43-32 lead and Buffalo pushed it to 44-32 with an extra point to make it a two-touchdown lead with 2:35 left.

#2764. Kalshi daily volume of ‘perps’ trading begins to top its prediction markets

Kalshi is known for its prediction markets across sports, politics and culture.

But now, for the first time, perpetual futures, Kalshi's second financial product, has surpassed the notional trading volume of its event contracts on consecutive days.

On Thursday, perpetual futures — commonly known as "perps" — on Kalshi saw $3.4 billion in notional volume as artificial intelligence laboratory Anthropic stock debuted on public markets while the rest of the market sold off. That exceeded about $3 billion in notional volume for Kalshi's prediction markets on the same day.

Perps are futures-style contracts where there is no expiration and traders do not need to own the underlying asset. Contracts instead continuously track the price of an asset, with funding payments to keep the contract price aligned with that of the market. Funding payments are made periodically between holders of short or long positions.

Perps trading surpassed event contracts one day in September, but on Friday perps topped prediction markets volume for a second day. Now Kalshi expects that performance to continue as it introduces perps tied to more individual stocks. On Monday, for example, Kalshi launched contracts linked to Anthropic.

Kalshi was the first to debut perps in the U.S. after winning federal regulatory approval in May to list perps tied to cryptocurrencies.

Perps trading volume includes any leverage that speculators acquire along with their trade, while event contracts are fully collateralized — for now.

Since the original launch in May, Kalshi has received approval to introduce perps tied to metals, crude oil, stock indexes and individual stocks.

Offshore perps — those available on exchanges not regulated in the U.S. — generated more than $90 trillion in notional trading volume in 2025, so it's not surprising that the product has grown so rapidly on Kalshi, according to Udesh Jha, chief risk officer at Kalshi Klear, the company's clearing house.

"We're just in very early innings. We're launching products, we're growing the participant base."

Perps were Kalshi's first product to mark its transition from a prediction market-focused platform to a full-blown financial exchange where various assets are traded. Last week, reports said that Kalshi is planning to offer trading on equities on its platform in the coming months.

At the same time, the company's perps product has come under fire. Some incumbent exchanges where futures are traded — which saw their stock prices decline following the approval of U.S. perps — believe the product was improperly approved. CME Group is suing the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal derivatives regulator, for approving perps.

Others have simply expressed concerns about the efficiency of the markets. CNBC previously reported that trading volume on Kalshi's most popular perps markets — tied to bitcoin and ether cryptocurrencies — was dramatically greater than open interest, or cash actively in the markets. It's a phenomenon that isn't common with U.S.-regulated traditional futures, nor other offshore perps markets.

Kalshi said at the time it has "zero concerns" about the distinction, and that it appears because less leverage is offered compared to offshore exchanges and there are fewer incentives for traders to park capital on its perps markets.

While Kalshi started with cryptocurrencies, Owen Lau, an analyst at Clear Street, said perps tied to individual stocks likely represent the most promising opportunity for growth in the U.S.

"That's how I foresee the way perps will penetrate further into traditional asset classes," Lau said. "Perps on single stocks and [key performance indicators on individual companies] could change how investors hedge their portfolio or even make a directional bet."

Kalshi's growth has been powered by the fact that right now it's the only exchange that can actively list perps in the U.S., though crypto exchange Coinbase received approval in May to route U.S. users to offshore platforms for trading. That monopoly status, however, will probably prove short lived.

Coinbase has filed to list single-stock perps in the U.S., and President Donald Trump in August said the CFTC is working to bring Hyperliquid — a dominant player in offshore perps activity — to domestic traders.

But Jha at Kalshi isn't worried about the competition.

"What will carry us is ... making sure that we have the right products for the marketplace, making sure we have the right surveillance of the products that we are trading."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2763. NFL Urged to Make Massive Change Amid Rashee Rice Catch Controversy

Kansas City Chiefs wide receiver Rashee Rice appeared to haul in a potentially game-changing catch late in the team's game against the Buffalo Bills on Thanksgiving, but a late review took it away.

Rice snagged a catch on 3rd-and-10 deep in Chiefs territory and the score tied at 10-10, which would have keep the Chiefs moving toward a potentially game-winning score. The review found that his hand lost contact with the ball when it hit the turf, overturning the catch and forcing the Chiefs to punt.

The call has generated significant controversy, with one reporter suggesting the NFL revert back to a more simple interpretation of the catch rule.

Rice said after the game that he believed he held onto the ball, a sentiment echoed by Chiefs head coach Andy Reid. Bills reporter Sal Capaccio acknowledged the confusion of the rule, noting that the NFL used to employ a more straightforward rule for surviving the turf.

"Up until the 2000 season, any ball that hit the ground at all during the process of a catch was ruled incomplete, regardless of any control," Capaccio shared in a post on X. "The Bert Emmanuel Rule changed that after the 1999 NFC Championship Game controversy. I understand the point, but have always hated the rule….not because it doesn't make sense, but because it brought too much subjectivity to the process."

Capaccio suggested the NFL could remove the current confusion by going back to the original rule — which still would have made Rice's catch incomplete.

"I'd like the NFL to just go back to that rule – if the ball touches the ground at all during the process of the catch, it's incomplete. Period. End of story," Capaccio wrote. "Keep it off the ground. Make it easier on everyone to see and know."

The Rice catch was not the only play that drew controversy in Kansas City's loss to Buffalo. There also appeared to be an unexplained addition of a few seconds on the final Chiefs drive, giving the Bills critical seconds that allowed them to attemp — and make — the game-winning kick.

After a fumbled snap, quarterback Patrick Mahomes appeared to be brought down with 19 seconds remaining in the fourth quarter, but the Bills called timeout and the clock was wound back to 25 seconds.

Officials did not explain why so much more time was added, and many fans expressed anger and confusion.

"The Bills clock guy should be banned for life," one person shared in a post on X. "The Chiefs started that drive with 26 seconds to go. The Mahomes snap, fumble and the whistle was called a ONE SECOND PLAY. The actual whistle went at 23 seconds and Brady can't have called a TO exactly then. 25 sec for 2nd down"

In the fourth quarter of Westchester's 125-104 win, Dillingham was punched by Capital City center Felix Okpara at the top of the 3-point line. Dillingham pushed Okpara on his way down the court, and the center looked at the official for a foul call.

When one wasn't made, Okpara clotheslined Dillingham across the neck and shoulders, knocking him to the floor.

Both teams then gathered in a scrum past the Westchester basket, going off the court as Okpara backpedaled toward the baseline. Coaches, officials and arena security personnel then separated each group of players.

Ermal Kuqo, the Knicks' senior director of minor league operations, and his 6-foot-10, 270-pound frame also got involved, which surely helped provide a significant obstacle to fisticuffs.

Dillingham and Okpara were subsequently both ejected from the game. Dillingham finished with 10 points and 8 assists, while Okpara scored 12 points with 9 rebounds for the Go-Go.

Ardisson said his supporters' club had continued to grow in popularity [BBC]

The Nashville Blue Army watches Town's games together in a pub in the city [Contributed]

#2756. Aaron Rodgers got no support from the Thanksgiving broadcasts

The day after former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers went on TV and aired dirty laundry from the Pittsburgh locker room, none of the former quarterbacks (or other former players) who appear on TV supported him.

Across the board, the reaction from the NFL’s analysts and broadcasters was negative.

Former Steelers quarterback and Fox analyst Terry Bradshaw went off. Fox’s Michael Strahan agreed that Rodgers shouldn’t have gone public with his complaints — calling him a “narcissist” and a “rich jerk” and a “rich bad teammate.”

Fox’s Howie Long said that the moment “defines who [Rodgers] is.”

“He’s a guy that I think picked the wrong sport,” Long said. “He probably should have played tennis.”

Fox’s Rob Gronkowski said that, even if a contender loses its starting quarterback, there’s “too much smoke” to justify bringing Rodgers in.

The CBS pregame crew offered similar views. Nate Burleson said Rodgers “didn’t handle this right.” Bill Cowher said he hates to see a Hall of Famer go out this way. Russell Wilson said, “It’s about Aaron Rodgers’s legacy. . . . If I’m his family members, if I’m somebody close to him, I’m his significant other, whatever it may be. ‘Is this how you want to go out?’ After all the success he’s had, is this how you want to go out? . . . This is a disappointment.”

During the Eagles-Cowboys game, Fox’s Tom Brady criticized Rodgers for violating the “sanctity” of the locker room.

The irony, if I’m using the term correctly (I never know), is that Rodgers has previously railed about leaks from the locker room.

“I think it’s chickenshit at its core, and I think it has no place in a winning organization,” Rodgers said about the Jets.

“One thing I really love — and it’s kind of the antithesis of where I was — is there’s not really any leaks in the boat,” Rodgers told reporters during his first year with the Steelers. “Every year, you have difficulties and adversity, both on the field and off the field, and to go through a season like this, and to be able to focus on football and not have a lot of other little bullshit out there has been really nice.”

Rodgers didn’t leak anything. He said it directly, bluntly, unapologetically. He took the team’s focus off of football, at a time when the team was otherwise trying to fight through “difficulties and adversity.”

Again, he isn’t a whistleblower. He’s a troublemaker. He didn’t get his way, so he set the house on fire as he walked out the front door.

As one PFT reader put it, ultimatums and “blackmail” are close cousins. For Rodgers, it wasn’t just “let me call the plays or cut me.” It was, whether he said it this way or not, “let me call the plays or I’ll tell the world all the stuff you don’t want the world to know.”

#2754. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2753. UW WBB: Tilda Trygger Showing Value For No. 16 Washington Huskies

The No. 16 Washington Huskies women's basketball team has made it through the first seven games of the season without a blemish on its resume.

Those seven wins have included a 50-plus win against the Stanford Cardinal on Nov. 20 and an 11-point win against the Oklahoma State Cowgirls on Friday as part of the Coconut Hoops Classic.

Entering the season, the Huskies had a unique benefit that they hadn't had in head coach Tina Langley's previous five seasons at the helm.

Junior forward Tilda Trygger transferred to Washington from North Carolina State. Her unique size (6-foot-6), allowed Langley to go with a two-post starting lineup for the first time in her head coaching tenure, and provided the team a size advantage over the majority of teams they'll face this season.

Trygger has worked this season to utilize her size in the paint more efficiently. While she's done that, she's also been effective at stretching the floor.

In addition to her game inside the post, Trygger also can shoot outside of it, including from 3-point range.

Trygger has displayed that skill set efficiently over the team's last four games.

Over UW's last four games from Nov. 12-27, Trygger has averaged 12.8 points and shot 60% (6-for-10) from 3-point range. She had 13 points on 5-for-7 shooting (1-for-1 from beyond the arc), brought in nine rebounds, dished five assists, had three blocks and a steal against Oklahoma State on Friday.

For the season, Trygger's averaging 11 points on 55.8% shooting (57.1% from 3-point range) across seven games. She's also averaged 7.6 boards, 1.7 assists and just under a block per contest.

"Tilda's been awesome to play with," junior UW guard Avery Howell said after a win against the Cal Poly Mustangs on Nov. 22. "Not even basketball-wise, just her personality. Just the leadership she brings. I think (it's) a really good balance for our team. She plays with a lot of joy, always. Sometimes some of us can get a little intense but she always kind of balances us out. It's just awesome to be on the court with her."

Trygger's presence allows the kind of flexibility in UW's lineup and roster that it hasn't had in previous seasons. Which, at times, has prevented the Huskies from taking the next step forward and prevailing against some of the top programs in the Big Ten, despite being competitive.

As long as the junior forward's play continues at its current form, then all those factors could help lead the team to taking the step forward and being legitimate Big Ten contenders.

Trygger and Washington will next be in action in its second game of the Coconut Hoops Classic at 10 a.m. PT on Sunday at the Alico Arean in Fort Myers, Fla.
