#2693. Undrafted rookie could be the Rams best option at tight end

After making a move towards multiple tight ends late last season, it was not surprising that the Los Angeles Rams brought eight tight ends into OTAs and kept them all through training camp. L.A. finally decided to keep five, a higher than average number, on the opening roster. They also kept around another tight end, prospect Dan Villari, on the practice squad.

Spring forward to the end of November and the Rams deep tight end room has been thinned substantially by injuries. Terrance Ferguson is out for the year; Colby Parkinson has missed one full game, parts of another half-dozen, and obviously impaired in many others; Davis Allen has a high ankle sprain, which carries a normal recovery time of a minimum of four weeks; and now Tyler Higbee has been designated as week-to-week with a bruised chest. Against the Green Packers on Thanksgiving Eve, the Rams installed offensive tackle David Quessenberry as tight end in the "13" personnel groupings.

Villari, 24 years-old, was signed by the Rams as a 2026 undrafted free agent out of Syracuse, but his journey to the NFL did not begin as a tight end. Originally recruited and signed as a quarterback by Michigan, he spent two seasons with the Wolverines. After a redshirt, he appeared in four 2021 games, recording one completion in three attempt and running for 35 yards on nine carries.

Transferred to Syracuse for 2022 and the Orangemen converted him to tight end in summer workouts. Over four seasons, he played in 44 games with 25 starts, catching 64 passes for 671 yards. In 2023 and 24, Syracuse, lined him up all over the field, collecting reps at running back and quarterback to go along with tight end duties. For his career, he chipped in 484 rushing yards at a 4.9 per carry clip and completed 23 of 33 passes for 177 yards.

It's fair to label him as a receiver first prospect. At 6'4" 243 lb.Villari is not a towering tight end like the rest of the unit. He sports just-above average arm length at 32 1/4" and owns good-sized hands, 9 3/4". On film, Villari looks like a fluid athlete. At his Pro Day, he didn't test in any of the explosive/strength drills, vertical, broad jump, or bench press. Bit of a discrepancy in the reporting of his forty yard dash times, RAS reports 4.77 with a 1.66 10-yard split, while others put him at a hand-timed 4.60. A 7.04 3cone shows stellar agility and flexibility. Burst and balance were excellent as well, 4.31 shuttle.

College film shows, even with his lack of experience, the looks of a natural fit at tight end. Primarily lined up in the slot, he gets to his spot and gives his quarterback a nice square target. Nice feel for open space. I have to describe his hands as iron in velvet, soft because the football just seems to be absorbed into his grip and strong because he plucks passes with his hands away from his body and tucks them away quickly. Solid wingspan and body control to haul in awry balls. Fearless over the middle and catches well through contact. As an open-field runner, I'd use "competitive" as the descriptor, he gets north/south in a hurry and runs low behind his pads, often right through first contact, Blocking needs a lot of technique and strength work. He's willing, but doesn't have much pop in his strikes and lacks the needed handwork/arm extension.

Came into the pros as a developmental prospect, but you can see he has the base traits. Villari has been on the receiving end of passes just three short years. He offers interesting versatility, although it would be highly suspect if the Rams would build a package of plays around an undrafted player joining the team this late in proceedings. He did however, run the ball out of wildcat formation, throws pretty well, and has very good hands. Like almost all youngsters, he needs route-running polish, but Villari's short area agility and natural move skills hint that he can handle a bigger route tree. Most of his targets were of the simpler variety, pivots, look-in/curls, and up the seams. In actuality, the current L.A. tight ends don't appear to be running a particularly complicated route tree either.

The Rams preseason lack of urgency in adding wide receiver depth hinted they're were going to make use of tight ends in the slot akin to receivers as a regular part of the rotation. Things have since changed due to all the injuries. Of the remaining two fully-available tight ends remaining, Parkinson can, and has been split out, but he's a bit heavy-footed and best suited closer to the action, while Max Klare is more natural to the slot, in skills, size, and athletically. Villari game appears more apt to fit in with Klare's.

Circumstances have certainly conspired against the Rams ball catchers, both wide outs and tight ends. Sean McVay would really be up against it if he were to try shapeshifting the offense again, back into three wide receiver sets. There doesn't appear to be many answers there. The L.A. braintrust looks limited in their options. At tight end, they will likely need to add a body. It could be a street retread or giving Villari a shot.

At $45k guaranteed, Villari was the Rams highest paid undrafted rookie in 2026, there must be more than "roster filler" interest, Yes indeed, he joined the team needing development and strength/conditioning work, but he's now been in the program for seven months, working on strength, technique and getting a grasp off the playbook. There is likely little or no upgrade available at this point.

In theory, McVay and tight ends coach Scott Huff could kick the tires on Villari, he has three practice squad elevations to the active roster, before a permanent decision needs to be made on his future. Elevated or signed, the Rams need to activate Dan Villari.

#2689. Heat 108, Knicks 105: “Really tough to win a poorly coached, corruptly reffed, half rotation injured game.”

The Knicks (12-7) lost two Emirates NBA Cup games this season but defeated the Cavaliers on Wednesday. Their chances of reaching the next round and continuing their defense of the Cup weren't nonexistent, but they needed to beat the Heat (11-7) by more than six points and for the Pacers to drop the Sixers.

First things first: the surging Heat are a handful even when New York's roster is complete. The Knicks had to meet the challenge without OG Anunoby (calf), Andre Drummond (concussion), Miles McBride (groin), and Jordan Clarkson (foot). The remaining players weren't altogether healthy, either: Karl-Anthony Towns (19 PTS, 10 RBS, 34 MIN) has an abdominal contusion, and Josh Hart (6 PTS, 9 RBS, 8 AST) is playing through a sore knee and shoulder. Our depleted Knickerbockers fell behind in the first quarter, then went down by 23 in the second. They cut their deficit to five in the third frame and had the Heat on the ropes in crunch time. A few missed free throws, an uncalled flagrant foul, and odd coaching decisions let the rally—and their Cup dreams—slip through their fingers. Final score: 108-105.

Quoth DWLLTheKnicksfan: "Really tough to win a poorly coached, corruptly reffed, half rotation injured game. Respect that it was close."

After the Knicks fell behind 15-7, coach Mike Brown replaced Hart with Pacôme Dadiet (4 PTS, 1 STL, 21 MIN, +7). Pac-Man played arm-flapping defense that disrupted the flow and led to a Miami turnover, then dunked a Brunson pass on the run. We're starting to see the vision! The French fella stayed on the floor when the next round of swaps came, playing alongside Brunson, Hart, Jose Alvarado (7 PTS, 15 MIN), and James Wiseman (11 PTS). The latter lasted just two minutes and committed a foul that heralded the return of Towns. Not a great first look on a night when Drummond was out.

New York logged nine boards in the first quarter. Giannis Antetokounmpo (22 PTS, 23 RBS, 32 MIN) matched that total alone, scarfing up most of Miami's 14 boards. A physical game had Towns wincing in pain, and we were wincing at New York's crappy shooting (2-of-11 from deep). With the Floridians sinking half of their three-pointers, the Knicks trailed 31-21 at the break.

In the second quarter, Mohamed Diawara and the Tylers (Kolek, Nickel) took the floor. Miami scored 12 unanswered while the 'Bockers continued to shoot like the Blind Boys of Alabama. The rookie Nickel looked lost and the coach bewildered. Mike Brown called for time when the score reached 48-27.

Out of that timeout, Brown sent Wiseman and Dadiet back in. Wiseman's minutes were rough. On consecutive plays, Wiggins picked off his inbound pass for a layup, and then Ryan Conwell intercepted his NEXT inbound pass. Unsurprisingly, he suffered a quick hook. Mike was running out of levers to pull as his team fell behind by 23. With four rotation players out and his starters all posting a plus-minus of -13 or worse, the coach fielded 11 different players in the first half in an attempt to slow Erik Spoelstra's squad, which scored inside, outside, and wherever else it wanted.

Thanks to a spirited late-half nine-oh run, the home team entered halftime trailing 60-44. There was plenty of blame to go around for our heroes. Towns (still painin'!) and Brunson combined for 25 of their team's 44 points, but the other starters had five points each, and the Knicks' bench had been outscored by its Miami counterpart, 30-4.

Through the half, the Flamingos shot 45% from the field and 36% from deep (9-of-25), while New York managed 40% and a miserable 21% (4-of-19). The Heat held only slight advantages in rebounds (24-23) and points in the paint (22-18) but were considerably better at protecting the ball. Nine Knicks turnovers yielded 11 Miami points, while the Heat committed just four turnovers that yielded three New York points. Worse, Miami won the battle for second-chance points, 10-0.

Over the first two minutes of the second half, Towns, Bridges, and Shamet combined for nine points to cut the deficit to 61-53. Miami fought back, but New York looked rejuvenated and alert (a marked improvement from the tryptophan blues of the first half). By the midway point of the quarter, the Knicks had converted five straight shots, reducing their deficit to five. As we've seen in game after game this season, it takes a lot of energy to climb out of deep holes. As the Knicks faltered, back-to-back Bridges turnovers allowed Giannis and Bam Adebayo (25 PTS, 7 RBS) to hammer Miami's lead back to 11.

Our heroes got an unexpected lift from Wiseman. He leaped into the fray, scoring seven points on three buckets and a free throw late in the third. That was more like it! Add a Brunson floater and an Antetokounmpo shot-clock violation, and New York trailed 86-79 at the horn.

Bam was a bother to start the fourth, netting two buckets, blocking shots by Shamet and Kolek, and drawing two fouls from Towns. Towns headed to the bench with four fouls at the 8:27 mark. Meanwhile, Shamet, Bridges, and Kolek each contributed layups to keep the Knicks within striking distance. The Heat struggled from the perimeter, with OAKAAKUYOAKs Tim Hardaway Jr. and Bobby Portis blowing opportunities to extend the lead.

Down 91-85 with plenty of game remaining, New York scored seven unanswered points, fueled by the bench mob. Wednesday's hero, Alvarado, scored five consecutive points, including a step-back three off his own offensive rebound, while Dadiet contributed a steal and a reverse layup off Brunson's eighth assist to give the Knicks their first lead. Davion Mitchell finally broke Miami's scoring drought with a floater, putting the Heat ahead 93-92 with six minutes remaining.

At the two-minute mark, the good guys trailed by three points after two freebies by Shamet. From there, Giannis launched an ill-advised, deep airball late in the shot clock; Brunson and Pelle Larsson traded free throws; Hart made one of two at the stripe; and Adebayo made the differential four with 44 seconds left. Captain Clutch's floater made it 104-102. Giannis missed two free throws with 14 seconds on the clock, and Adebayo fouled Wiseman on the loose ball, giving our heroes a soupçon of hope. My bingo card did not have Wiseman shooting free throws with the game on the line. How about yours?

The young journeyman made one, missed one, and Giannis snagged the board and called a timeout. 104-103, 13 seconds to go. When play resumed, Dadiet fouled Mitchell, who made both attempts. On the Knicks' inbounds play, the ball swung to Brunson in the corner, where he was fouled by Conwell. Even though Conwell violated JB's landing space, Scott "The Enforcer" Foster deemed it a common foul. Cap made two of three at the line. Spoelstra called a timeout. 106-105, nine seconds.

Larsson got a breakaway dunk—on the exact same inbounds play the Heat had run on their previous possession. Mike Brown elected not to call a timeout, and Brunson missed a contested triple to end the game.

This is your recap, chaps. As for the Knicks, they'll sleep in their own beds for a few more days and host the Hawks on Sunday. Rest up, troops.

#2687. Ex-UFC Champion Makes Bold Suggestion for Conor McGregor’s Next Opponent

LAS VEGAS, NEVADA - JULY 11: Conor McGregor of Ireland looks on prior to facing Max Holloway of the United States in their welterweight bout during UFC 329 at T-Mobile Arena on July 11, 2026 in Las Vegas, Nevada. (Photo by Ian Maule/Getty Images) Getty

Former two-division UFC champion Henry Cejudo made a bold suggestion for Conor McGregor's next opponent. Cejudo made a strong case for reigning lightweight champion Justin Gaethje to face McGregor upon his return.

McGregor is currently recovering from his leg injury. The injury occurred during the opening exchange of his return fight against Max Holloway at UFC 329. It was unfortunate for the Irishman, as it marked his first fight in five years.

McGregor is eager to return to the octagon and fight on the final bout remaining on his current UFC contract. He has expressed interest in running it back with Holloway, but the UFC matchmakers will ultimately make the decision.

While McGregor being awarded a lightweight title shot is unlikely, Cejudo revealed how McGregor vs. Gaethje could become a reality.

Henry Cejudo suggested that the UFC book a fight between Conor McGregor and Justin Gaethje. One of the main reasons for his suggestion is his respect for Gaethje, who has always come through for the promotion.

'The Highlight' always puts on exciting fights and Cejudo feels that he should be rewarded with a lucrative fight. Cejudo also pointed to McGregor's contract status and is confident that Gaethje would have a favorable matchup.

With McGregor potentially leaving the promotion, 'Triple C' backed Gaethje in a potential bout to ensure the Irishman does not leave with a win.

"I think there's a big fight potentially – and Kamaru Usman brought this up, so I'm going to credit Kamaru on this one – [Gaethje] and Conor McGregor. No, I don't think for [the lightweight championship]. I think Justin deserves a big payday and I think Conor McGregor is going, it's his last fight, and I do believe that the UFC believes that Justin could beat him. So, I think you do that," Cejudo told MMA Fighting.

"I think [Gaethje could] relinquish the belt and who knows, man, maybe since Dana White loves [Alexander] Volkanovski so much, maybe he should do Arman [Tsarukyan] vs. Alexander Volkanovski [for the vacant lightweight title]."

GettyLAS VEGAS, NEVADA – JULY 11: Conor McGregor of Ireland looks on prior to facing Max Holloway of the United States in their welterweight bout during UFC 329 at T-Mobile Arena on July 11, 2026 in Las Vegas, Nevada. (Photo by Ian Maule/Getty Images)

If the UFC were to book McGregor vs. Gaethje, the bout would most likely be contested at welterweight.

McGregor's latest bout against Holloway, who competes at lightweight, was also contested at welterweight. Fighting Gaethje at 170 pounds ensures that 'The Notorious' does not have to worry about cutting weight or being undersized.

Gaethje could very easily compete at 170 pounds, which could give him a break from having to cut weight as well.

Additionally, contesting the fight at welterweight would also ensure that the lightweight division does not get held up. Gaethje could vacate the title and move up to face McGregor. If he wins and decides to continue fighting, he could return to lightweight for one more fight.

#2684. Rapid Recap: Pistons 113, Bucks 98

The Milwaukee Bucks started extremely well, but the Detroit Pistons' defence—which sits atop the league without Cade Cunningham—proved too much to overcome, and the Bucks lost 113-98. Brayden Burries led the Bucks with 23 points and seven rebounds, while Isaiah Joe starred for the Pistons with 22 points amid a spread of contributors.

The Bucks got off to a ripping start on both ends, taking advantage of the weapons the Pistons had out, running on misses to build a 10-2 lead and forcing an early timeout. Clearly, the timeout didn't do much, as Milwaukee stuck to their game plan, forcing Detroit into tougher shots and playing even more in transition as Kyle Kuzma, Jaime Jaquez Jr., and Ryan Rollins starred early for a combined 17 points—Bucks up 19-3 six minutes in! The Pistons finally got into gear after that as their bench filtered in, going on an 11-3 run as Tyler Herro and Myles Turner lost their men on the perimeter. Milwaukee would right the ship, though, as Brayden Burries checked in and scored a quick seven points to put the Bucks up 27-14 with two minutes to play. Unfortunately, some late mistakes by Milwaukee's youth allowed the Pistons to close on a 10-2 run, with the Bucks up 29-24 after one.

The Bucks started the second quarter with the same brand of silly basketball they finished the first with, repeatedly turning it over and taking bad shots as they went scoreless for nearly five minutes and let the Pistons take the lead. If not for Detroit missing a bunch of open shots, Milwaukee would have been in some strife. Luckily for the visitors, the game became a slog, and they stayed close, with neither team surpassing the 40-point mark until there was about three minutes to go. Yeah, it was ugly. Tyler Herro finally made his first field goal shortly thereafter, giving the Bucks a two-point edge, which they took into the locker room, up 50-48 at the half.

The Pistons came out ready and raring to go in the third, starting with an 11-4 run as Ausar Thompson began to make his mark. Both teams finally began to flow on offence as the Pistons nursed a 5-8-point advantage over the next few minutes, but a Bucks push cut the deficit to two, 71-69, with four to play. Milwaukee played decent half-court defence, but the minute they turned it over in live play—which they did a lot—Detroit almost always converted. That was, of course, until the final few minutes of the third, when the Pistons went bonkers no matter how they got their shots up, dominating on both ends as they closed on a 19-7 run—which included a buzzer-beating transition heave from Daniss Jenkins—to lead 90-76 after three.

Detroit kept turning up the defensive intensity early in the fourth, and with Tyler Herro resting, Milwaukee had nobody to calm them down and generate half-decent shots. Then we got some fireworks: Ausar Thompson beat Myles Turner down low—which he had done all night—shoved him in the chest, and the two came to blows, earning double technical fouls after Turner threw the ball at Thompson. From there, Detroit skied downhill as the Bucks struggled even to get shots up, and the deficit expanded to 20 points by the four-minute mark. It was all academic from that point forward as the wounded Pistons rolled over the Bucks.

I mean, to me it's just the Bucks' shooting percentage of 43.2%. What Detroit can do defensively, no matter who is sitting, is truly special.

The Bucks may never shoot well from three again.
